# P R E F A C E .

<image-desc>A circular library stamp on the right side of the page, showing a shield-like crest in the center.</image-desc>

I wrote this <term>Treatise</term><note>French: "Traité"; likely referring to his "Treatise on Light."</note> during my stay in France twelve years ago; and I shared it in the year 1678 with the learned individuals who then composed the <term>Royal Academy of Sciences</term><note>The French national scientific institution founded in 1666 by King Louis XIV.</note>, to which the King had done me the honor of calling me. Several members of this body who are still living may remember being present when I read it aloud, and better than the others, those among them who applied themselves particularly to the study of Mathematics; of whom I can now cite only the celebrated Gentlemen **Cassini**, **Roemer**, and **De la Hire** <note>Giovanni Domenico Cassini, Ole Rømer, and Philippe de La Hire were all world-renowned astronomers and mathematicians of the 17th century.</note>. And although I have since corrected and changed several parts of it, the copies I had made at that time could serve as proof that I have nonetheless added nothing to it, except for some conjectures concerning the formation of **Iceland Crystal** <note>original: "Cristal d’Islande"; now known as Iceland spar or calcite, famous for its double refraction.</note> and a new observation on the refraction of **Rock Crystal** <note>original: "Cristal de Roche"; clear quartz.</note>. I wanted to recount these details to make known for how long I have meditated upon these things

<summary>In this preface, Christiaan Huygens establishes the timeline of his work, noting that he first presented his theories on light to the French Royal Academy of Sciences in 1678.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Royal Academy of Sciences, Cassini, Roemer, De la Hire, Iceland spar, quartz, refraction, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion regarding the author’s long-term meditation on his optical theories. The catchword "fes" from the previous page concludes the word "choses" (things) which begins this page.</meta>

# P R E F A C E .

things that I publish now, and not to detract from the merit of those who, without having seen anything of what I had written, may have found themselves treating similar matters: as indeed happened to two <term>excellent geometers</term><note>In the 17th century, "geometer" was a common term for a mathematician.</note>, Messrs. **Newton** and **Leibniz**, regarding the problem of the shape of lenses for focusing rays when one of the surfaces is given.

One might ask why I have delayed so long in bringing this work to light. The reason is that I had written it rather carelessly in the language in which it is seen<note>referring to French, the language of the original publication, rather than Latin which was the standard for scientific works at the time.</note>, with the intention of translating it into **Latin**, doing so to give closer attention to the subject matter. After which I proposed to provide it along with another <term>Treatise on Dioptrics</term><note>Dioptrics is the branch of optics that deals with the refraction of light through lenses.</note>, where I explain the effects of <term>telescopes</term>, and what else pertains to that science. But the pleasure of novelty having ceased, I have postponed the execution of this design from time to time, and I do not know when I might yet have completed it, being often distracted either by business or by some new study. Considering this, I finally judged that it was better to let this writing appear as it is, rather than to let it run the risk, by waiting longer, of remaining lost.

One will see in it those kinds of demonstrations, which

<summary>Huygens explains that his delay in publishing was due to a lost interest in the "novelty" of the work and a failed plan to translate it into Latin. He acknowledges that contemporaries like Newton and Leibniz independently reached similar mathematical conclusions during this hiatus.</summary>
<keywords>publication delay, Latin translation, Newton, Leibniz, lens shape, Dioptrics, scientific methodology</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page: "One will see in it those kinds of demonstrations, which..." The catchword "la" at the bottom of the page indicates that the following page will begin with that word, likely continuing the description of "Light" (la Lumiere).</meta>

# P R E F A C E .

do not produce as great a certainty as those of **Geometry**, and even differ from them greatly; for whereas Geometers prove their Propositions by certain and incontestable Principles, here the Principles are verified by the conclusions drawn from them; the nature of these matters not allowing it to be done otherwise. It is possible, however, to arrive at a degree of <term>probability</term><note>The original French uses *vraisemblance* (likelihood). Huygens is describing the "hypothetico-deductive" method, where a theory is proven by how well it explains observations rather than by mathematical first principles.</note> that often yields very little to total certainty. This occurs when the things demonstrated by these assumed Principles relate perfectly to the <term>phenomena</term><note>In this context, "phenomena" refers to observable events in nature, specifically the behavior of light.</note> that experience has noted; especially when there are a great number of them, and even more so when one conceives of and predicts *new phenomena* that must follow from the **hypotheses** being used, and finds that the outcome meets our expectations. 

If all these proofs of probability are found in what I have proposed to treat—as it seems to me they are—it must be a very great confirmation of the success of my research; and it is unlikely that things are not very nearly as I represent them. I would therefore believe that those who love to know the **Causes**, and who know how to admire the marvel of **Light**, will find some satisfaction in these various speculations which

<summary>Huygens defends his scientific method, arguing that while physics lacks the absolute certainty of geometry, a hypothesis is validated when it successfully predicts and explains a wide range of observable phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>scientific method, probability, geometry, hypotheses, observation, nature of light, causes</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page: "I would therefore believe that those who love to know the Causes, and who know how to admire the marvel of Light, will find some satisfaction in these various speculations which..."</meta>

# P R E F A C E .

regard it, and in the new explanation of its <term>notable property</term><note>Huygens is referring to the laws of refraction and the way light waves spread, which he uses to explain how lenses and vision work.</note>, which forms the main foundation of the **structure of our eyes**, and of those great inventions that so greatly extend their use. I also hope that there will be some who, by following these beginnings, will penetrate further into this entire subject than I have been able to do, since it is far from being exhausted. This is evident from the places I have marked, where I leave difficulties without resolving them; and even more so by the things I have not touched upon at all, such as **Luminous Bodies**<note>Original: "Corps Luiſants." These are objects that produce their own light, like the sun or a flame, as opposed to objects that only reflect it.</note> of various kinds, and everything regarding **Colors**; in which no one until now can boast of having succeeded. Finally, there remains much more to seek concerning the nature of **Light** than I claim to have discovered, and I shall owe much in return to whoever can supply what I lack here in knowledge.

->At The Hague. January 8, 1690.<-

### CHAP. I. On rays traveling in a straight line.
That Light is produced by a certain motion. Page 2.
That no physical body passes from the luminous object to our eyes. p. 3.
That Light spreads spherically, and nearly like Sound. p. 4.
Whether Light takes time to spread. p. 4.
Experiment which seems to prove that it passes in an instant. p. 5.
Experiment which proves that it takes time. p. 7.
By how much its speed is greater than that of Sound. p. 9.
In what way the emanation of Light differs from that of Sound. p. 9.
That it is not the same medium that serves for both. p. 10.
How Sound spreads. p. 11.
How Light spreads. p. 12.
Particular remark on the extension of Light. p. 17.

How Light, coming from different places, crosses through itself without hindrance. p. 20.

Demonstration of the Equality of the angles of incidence and reflection. p. 21.
Why the incident and reflected rays are in the same plane, perpendicular to the reflecting surface. p. 24.
That it is not necessary for the reflecting surface to be perfectly smooth to achieve the equality of the angles of incidence and reflection. p. 25.

That bodies could be transparent without any matter passing

How this matter passing through them makes them transparent. p. 29.
That the most solid-looking bodies have a very sparse structure. p. 29.

What can make bodies opaque. p. 31.

Why the incident Ray and the refracted ray are produced reciprocally. p. 36.

That the emanations of Light in the air are not spherical. p. 43.
How, because of this, some objects appear higher than they are. p. 44.
How the Sun can appear on the Horizon before it has risen. p. 45.
That rays of Light become curved in the Air of the Atmosphere, and what effects this produces. p. 46.

# T R E A T I S E
## O N  L I G H T.
### C H A P. I.
### ON RAYS TRAVELING IN STRAIGHT LINES.

The proofs concerning <term>Optics</term><note>The branch of physics that studies the behavior and properties of light.</note>, as occurs in all the sciences where <term>Geometry</term> is applied to matter, are founded on truths drawn from experience; such as that rays of light extend in a straight line; that the angles of <term>reflection</term> and <term>incidence</term> are equal: and that in <term>refractions</term> the ray is bent according to the <term>Rule of Sines</term><note>Now commonly known as Snell’s Law, this formula describes how light bends when passing from one medium (like air) into another (like glass or water).</note>, which is now so well known, and which is no less certain than the preceding ones.

Most of those who have written concerning the various parts of Optics have been content to take these truths for granted. But some more inquisitive minds have sought to investigate their origin and causes, considering the truths themselves as wonderful effects of <term>Nature</term>. While they have put forward ingenious ideas, they are not yet such that the most intelligent readers do not wish for explanations that satisfy them more; I wish to propose here what I have meditated on this subject, to contribute as much as I can to the clarification of this part of <term>Natural Science</term>, which is considered, not without reason, to be one of its most difficult branches. I recognize that I am much indebted to those who first began to dispel the strange obscurity in which these thi-

<summary>This opening page of Huygens’ seminal work introduces the foundational principles of optics—rectilinear propagation, reflection, and refraction—and states his intent to provide a deeper mechanical explanation for these phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>Optics, Geometry, light rays, reflection, incidence, refraction, Rule of Sines, Natural Science, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Chapter I. The previous page ended mid-sentence with "clarification of t...", which is completed here by the word "things" (choses).</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

...things were shrouded, and to offer hope that they might be explained by intelligible reasons. On the other hand, I am also surprised how those very authors have often tried to pass off arguments that are hardly evident as being certain and demonstrative; for I find that no one has yet provided a probable explanation for these primary and notable phenomena of <term>light</term>, namely: why it only extends along straight lines, and how <term>visual rays</term>, coming from an infinite number of different places, cross one another without interfering with each other at all.

In this book, I shall therefore attempt, using principles accepted in today’s philosophy <note>Huygens refers to the "mechanical philosophy" prevalent in the 17th century, which sought to explain natural phenomena through the motion and collision of physical particles.</note>, to provide clearer and more plausible reasons: first, for these properties of <term>light</term> traveling in straight lines; second, for light that is <term>reflected</term> when meeting other bodies. Then, I will explain the characteristics of rays that are said to undergo <term>refraction</term> when passing through <term>diaphanous bodies</term><note>Materials that are transparent or allow light to pass through, such as glass or water.</note> of various kinds; where I will also treat the effects of the <term>refraction of air</term> caused by the differing densities of the <term>atmosphere</term>.

Next, I will examine the causes of the strange <term>refraction</term> of a certain <term>crystal</term> brought from <term>Iceland</term><note>Known today as "Iceland Spar" (a form of calcite), this crystal exhibits double refraction, a phenomenon where a single ray of light enters the crystal and splits into two.</note>. And finally, I will treat the various shapes of <term>transparent</term> and <term>reflecting bodies</term> by which rays are gathered at a point or diverted in different ways. There, it will be seen with what ease our new Theory allows us to find not only the <term>ellipses</term>, <term>hyperboles</term>, and other curved lines that <term>Mr. Descartes</term><note>René Descartes (1596–1650), whose work *Dioptrics* was the standard text on the subject before Huygens.</note> subtly invented for this purpose, but also those that must form the surface of a lens when the other surface is already given as spherical, flat, or of any other shape.

One cannot doubt that <term>light</term> consists of the motion of a certain type of matter. For whether one considers its produc-

He also introduces his intent to explain the "strange refraction" of Iceland Spar and improve upon Descartes' mathematical models for lens design.</summary>

## OF LIGHT. CHAP. I.

...production, one finds that here on Earth it is primarily fire and flame that create it, which undoubtedly contain bodies in rapid motion, since they dissolve and melt many other very solid bodies. Whether one observes its effects, one sees that when light is gathered together, as by concave mirrors, it has the power to burn just like fire—that is, it disunites the parts of bodies <note>In the 17th century, "disuniting the parts" meant breaking the physical bonds between the tiny particles that make up a solid object.</note>. This certainly indicates movement, at least in **true Philosophy**, in which the cause of all natural effects is conceived through mechanical reasoning. In my opinion, one must do this, or else give up all hope of ever understanding anything in **Physics**.

And as, following this **Philosophy**, it is held for certain that the sensation of sight is only stimulated by the impression of some movement of a matter that acts upon the nerves at the back of our eyes, this is yet another reason to believe that light consists of a movement of the matter found between us and the luminous body.

Furthermore, when one considers the extreme speed with which light spreads in all directions, and that when it comes from different places—even those completely opposite—the rays pass through one another without hindering each other; one understands well that when we see a luminous object, it cannot be by the transport of a matter which travels from that object to us, as a ball or an arrow crosses the air. For certainly, that contradicts those two qualities of light too much, and especially the latter. It is therefore in another manner that it spreads; and what can lead us to understand this is the knowledge we have of the spreading of <term>Sound</term><note>Huygens is famously introducing the "wave theory" of light here, using sound waves as an analogy for how light might travel through a medium.</note> in the air.

We know that by means of the air, which is an invisible and impalpable body, <term>Sound</term> spreads all around the place where it was produced, by a movement which passes successively...

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: of a</meta>

<summary>Huygens argues that light is a form of motion rather than a stream of particles, comparing its travel through a medium to the way sound moves through the air.</summary>
<keywords>light, mechanical philosophy, physics, vision, movement, sound, wave theory, particles</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that light travels through a medium rather than being a physical projectile. It transitions from an analogy with sound waves to the core question of whether light travels at a finite speed or instantaneously.</meta>

# TREATISE

from one part of the air to another, and because the extension of this movement occurs at an equal speed in all directions, it must form something like spherical surfaces, which expand continuously and eventually strike our ear. Now, there is no doubt that light also reaches us from a luminous body to our eyes by some movement impressed upon the matter that lies between the two; for we have already seen that it cannot be by the transport of a physical body passing from one to the other. If, in addition to this, light takes time for its passage—which we are now going to examine—it will follow that this movement impressed upon the matter is <term>successive</term><note>Huygens uses "successive" to mean that movement happens step-by-step over time, rather than happening everywhere at once (instantaneously).</note>, and that consequently it spreads, just like that of Sound, by spherical surfaces and waves. I call them **waves** because of their resemblance to those one sees forming in water when a stone is thrown into it, which represent such a successive spreading in a circle, although arising from a different cause and occurring only on a flat surface.

To see then if the extension of light occurs over time, let us first consider if there are any experiments that might convince us of the contrary. As for those that can be performed here on Earth with fires placed at great distances, although they prove that light takes no <term>sensible time</term><note>Meaning a duration of time that is perceptible to human senses or measurable by the instruments of the 1670s.</note> to cross these distances, one can rightly say that these distances are too small, and that one can only conclude that the passage of light is extremely fast. **Mr. Descartes**, who was of the opinion that light is instantaneous, based his view—not without reason—on a much better experiment drawn from **Lunar Eclipses**; which, however, as I shall show, is not convincing. I will propose the argument slightly differently than he did, to make the full consequence better understood.

Let **A** be the location of the sun, and **B D** be a part of the orbit or path—

It will therefore be one hour later, counting from the time the Earth was at B, that the Moon, arriving at C, will be darkened there. But this darkening or interruption of light will not reach the Earth for another hour. Let us suppose that in these two hours [the Earth] has reached E. The Earth, therefore, being at E, will see the Moon eclipsed at C, from which the light departed an hour before, and will see at the same time the Sun at A. But if one supposes that it requires only one minute of time for that, then it is manifest that the angle *C E G* will only be 33 minutes; and if it requires only ten seconds of time, this angle will not be [even] six minutes. And then it is not easy to notice it in eclipse observations, nor consequently permitted to conclude anything from it regarding the instantaneous movement of light.

It is true that this is to suppose a strange speed which would be a hundred thousand times greater than that of <term>Sound</term>. For Sound, according to what I have observed, travels about 180 <term>Toises</term><note>A *toise* is an old French unit of length, approximately 1.949 meters or 6.4 feet. This measurement suggests a speed of sound around 350 meters per second.</note> in the time of one Second or a heartbeat. But this supposition should not seem to have anything impossible [about it]; because it is not a matter of the transport of a body with such speed, but of a successive movement that passes from one [particle] to another. I have therefore found no difficulty, in meditating on these things, in supposing that the emanation of light occurred over time, seeing that by this all its phenomena could be explained, and that by following the contrary opinion everything was incomprehensible. For it has always seemed to me, and to many others with me, that even Mr. <term>Des Cartes</term><note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the famous philosopher and scientist. ### OF LIGHT. CHAP. I.

[treat]ment of all subjects of **Physics**, and who certainly succeeded much better therein than anyone before him, has said nothing that is not full of difficulties, or even inconceivable, regarding **Light** and its properties. <note>Huygens is likely referring to René Descartes, whose mechanical theory of light he both admired and sought to improve upon.</note>

But what I was using only as a hypothesis has recently received the strong appearance of a constant truth through the ingenious demonstration by **Mr. Rømer** <note>Ole Rømer (1644–1710), the first person to quantitatively measure the speed of light.</note> which I am going to report here, while waiting for him to provide everything that should serve to confirm it. It is founded, like the previous one, on celestial observations, and proves not only that light employs time in its passage, but also shows how much time it employs, and that its speed is at least six times greater than the one I just mentioned.

->T R E A T I S E<-

...and a half hours, one would see another such emergence; because this is the time in which it completes the circuit of its orbit, and returns to opposition with the Sun. And if the Earth remained always at B during 30 revolutions, for example, of this **satellite**, it would see it emerge from the shadow again after 30 times 42 and a half hours. But the Earth having moved during this time to C, while moving further away from *Jupiter*, it follows that if light takes time for its passage, the illumination of the little planet <note>Huygens refers to Jupiter's moon, likely Io, as a "little planet."</note> will be perceived later at C than it would have been at B; and that one must add, to this time of 30 times 42 and a half hours, also that which the light employs to cross the space MC, the difference of the distances CH and BH. Likewise, toward the other quadrature <note>A point in a planet's orbit where it appears 90 degrees away from the Sun as seen from Earth.</note> when the Earth has come from D to E, while approaching *Jupiter*, the immersions of the **satellite** G into the shadow must be observed earlier at E than they would have appeared if the Earth had remained at D.

Now, by a quantity of observations of these *eclipses*, made during ten consecutive years, these differences were found to be very significant, such as ten minutes or more; and it was concluded that to cross the entire diameter of the annual orbit KL <note>The "annual orbit" refers to the diameter of Earth's orbit around the Sun.</note>, which is double the distance from here to the Sun, l<unclear>ight</unclear> needs about 22 minutes of time. <note>Rømer’s original estimate of 22 minutes is slightly higher than the modern measurement of approximately 16.7 minutes for light to cross Earth's orbital diameter.</note>

The motion of *Jupiter* in its orbit, while the Earth passes from B to C or from D to E, is included in this calculation; and it is shown that one cannot attribute the delay of these illuminations, nor the anticipation of the *eclipses*, to the irregularity found in the motion of this little planet, nor to its eccentricity.

If one considers the vast extent of the diameter KL, which in my view is some 24 thousand diameters of the Earth, one will recognize the extreme speed of light. For supposing that KL is only 22 thousand of these diameters, it appears that—

### OF LIGHT. CHAPTER I.

...passed in 22 minutes, this makes one thousand diameters in a minute, and 16 2/3 diameters in a second or heartbeat <note>The "battement d’artère" (pulse-beat) was a common informal unit for a second in the 17th century.</note>, which amounts to more than eleven hundred times one hundred thousand toises <term>toise: an old French unit of length, roughly 1.949 meters or 6.4 feet</term>; since the diameter of the Earth contains 2,865 leagues (at 25 leagues per degree), and each league is 2,282 Toises, according to the exact measurement that Mr. Picard <note>Jean-Félix Picard (1620–1682), the French astronomer who first accurately measured the size of the Earth using triangulation.</note> took by order of the King in 1669. But **Sound**, as I have said before, only travels 180 toises in the same time of one second: therefore the **speed** of light is more than six hundred thousand times greater than that of **Sound**: which, however, is quite different from being instantaneous, since there is the same difference as between something finite and something infinite. Now, the successive movement of light being confirmed in this manner, it follows, as I have already said, that it spreads in **spherical waves**, just like the movement of Sound.

But if both resemble each other in that respect, they differ in several other things; namely in the initial production of the movement that causes them; in the matter through which this movement spreads; and in the manner in which it is communicated. For as far as the production of Sound is concerned, we know it is by the sudden shaking of an entire body, or of a considerable part, which agitates all the contiguous air. But the movement of light must originate, as it were, from every point of the **luminous object**, to be able to make all the different parts of that object perceptible, as will be better seen in what follows. And I do not believe that this movement can be better explained than by supposing those among the luminous bodies which are liquid, such as flame, and apparently the sun and the stars, to be composed of particles swimming in a much more subtle matter, which agitates them with great rapidity and makes them strike against the particles of the **ether**, which surround them and are much smaller than they are. But that in solid luminous bodies such as coal, or metal reddened in the fire, this same move-

<meta>This page continues the argument for light as a mechanical wave. Having previously established light's finite speed, Huygens now addresses the specific nature of the medium (the "ether") compared to the medium of sound (air), referencing Robert Boyle’s vacuum experiments to prove they are distinct.</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

movement is caused by the violent shaking of the particles of metal or wood, of which those on the surface strike the **ethereal matter** in the same way. Furthermore, the agitation of the particles that generate light must be much swifter and more rapid than that of the bodies which cause sound, since we do not see that the trembling of a sounding body is capable of giving rise to light, just as the movement of the hand in the air is not capable of producing Sound.

Now, if one examines what this matter might be in which the movement coming from luminous bodies spreads—which I call <term>Ethereal</term><note>Huygens refers to the "Aether," a hypothetical substance once thought to fill all space and allow light waves to travel through a vacuum.</note>—one will see that it is not the same matter that serves for the propagation of Sound. For we find that the latter is properly this air that we feel and that we breathe, which, being removed from a place, does not prevent the other matter that serves light from being found there. This is proven by enclosing a sounding body in a glass vessel, from which the air is then drawn by the machine that <term>Mr. Boyle</term><note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), the Anglo-Irish natural philosopher who improved the air pump to create vacuums for his experiments.</note> has given us, and with which he has performed so many beautiful experiments. But in performing the one of which I speak, one must take care to place the sounding body on cotton or on feathers, so that it cannot communicate its vibrations to the glass vessel that encloses it, nor to the machine—a detail which had until now been neglected. For then, after having emptied all the air, one hears no Sound from the metal even though it is struck.

One sees from this not only that our air, which does not penetrate the glass, is the matter through which Sound spreads; but also that it is not this same air, but another matter in which light spreads; since, the air being removed from this vessel, the light does not cease to pass through it as before.

# ON LIGHT. CHAP. I.

B 2

<meta>catchword: strai-</meta>

...the famous experiment of <term>Torricelli</term><note>Evangelista Torricelli (1608–1647), the Italian physicist who invented the barometer.</note>; where the glass tube, from which the <term>quicksilver</term><note>The historical name for mercury (original: *vif argent*).</note> has withdrawn, remaining entirely empty of air, transmits light just as it does when there is air present: for this proves that a matter different from air is found in this tube, and that this matter must have pierced the glass, or the quicksilver, or both, which are both impenetrable to air. And when, in the same experiment, a vacuum is created by putting a little water over the quicksilver, one concludes similarly that the said matter passes through the glass, or the water, or through both.

As for the different ways in which I said that the movements of **Sound** and of **light** are successively communicated, one can understand well enough how this occurs regarding **Sound** when one considers that air is of such a nature that it can be compressed and reduced to a space much smaller than it usually occupies; and that as it is compressed, it strives to expand again: for this, combined with its penetrability, which remains despite its compression, seems to prove that it is made of small bodies that swim and are agitated very quickly in the **ethereal matter**, composed of much smaller parts. Thus, the cause of the expansion of the waves of **Sound** is the effort made by these small bodies, which collide with one another, to expand again when they are a bit more crowded within the circuit of these waves than elsewhere.

But the extreme speed of **light**, and other properties it has, could not admit such a propagation of movement, and I will show here in what manner I conceive it must be. To do this, one must explain the property that hard bodies have in transmitting movement to one another.

When one takes a number of balls of equal size, made of some very hard material, and arranges them in a line

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens’ explanation of how movement is transmitted through matter. After discussing the "ethereal matter" in the previous page, he now uses a mechanical analogy—the collision of hard spheres—to explain how light can travel at extreme speeds through a medium without the medium itself being displaced over long distances. This is a foundational description of longitudinal wave propagation.</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

straight, so that they touch one another; one finds, by striking with a similar ball against the first of these balls, that the movement passes as if in an instant to the last one, which separates from the row without any visible movement in the others. Even the ball that struck remains motionless with them. Here we see a transmission of movement of extreme speed, which is all the greater as the material of the balls is of greater hardness.

But it is also certain that this progression of movement is not instantaneous, but successive, and thus it requires time. For if the movement—or, if one prefers, the inclination toward movement—did not pass successively through all these balls, they would all acquire it at the same time, and consequently, they would all move forward together; this does not happen. Instead, the last one leaves the entire row and acquires the speed of the one that was pushed. Furthermore, there are experiments showing that all those bodies we count among the hardest, such as tempered steel, glass, and **Agate**, are <term>elastic</term><note>Huygens uses the term *font ressort* (literally "act as a spring"), referring to the property of elasticity where a material deforms under pressure and then returns to its shape.</note>, and bend in some way, not only when they are stretched into rods, but also when they are in the form of balls or otherwise. That is to say, they yield slightly inward at the spot where they are struck and immediately return to their original shape. For I have found that by striking a ball of glass or **Agate** against a large and very thick piece of the same material, which had a flat surface slightly dulled with breath or otherwise, round marks remained, larger or smaller depending on whether the blow had been strong or weak. This shows that these materials yield upon impact and then restore themselves, for which they must employ time.

### OF LIGHT. CHAP. I.

...particles of the ether to be of a material so nearly approaching perfect hardness and of an <term>elasticity</term><note>Huygens uses the word *ressort* (spring). In 17th-century physics, this refers to the ability of particles to instantly snap back to their original shape after being compressed, a property we now call elasticity.</note> so prompt as we desire. It is not necessary here to examine the cause of this hardness, nor that of the elasticity, as such a consideration would lead us too far from our subject. I will say, however, in passing, that one can conceive that these particles of the ether, notwithstanding their smallness, are still composed of other parts, and that their elasticity consists in the very rapid movement of a <term>subtle matter</term><note>A hypothetical substance composed of extremely fine particles that Cartesian scientists believed filled all voids and transmitted forces through the universe.</note> which traverses them from all sides and constrains their texture to arrange itself in such a way that it gives the most open and easy passage possible to this fluid matter. This agrees with the reasoning that <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the influential French philosopher and mathematician who proposed that light was a pressure transmitted through a medium.</note>Mr. Descartes gives for elasticity, except that I do not suppose pores in the form of round, hollow canals as he does. And one must not imagine that there is anything absurd or impossible in this; it is, on the contrary, quite credible that it is this infinite progression of different sizes of <term>corpuscles</term><note>Small particles or physical atoms.</note> and the various degrees of their speed which Nature uses to produce so many marvelous effects.

But even if we were ignorant of the true cause of elasticity, we see constantly that there are many bodies which possess this property; and thus there is nothing strange in also supposing it to exist in small invisible bodies like those of the Ether. If one wishes to seek any other way in which the movement of light is communicated successively, one will find none that suits the uniform progression—which seems to be necessary—better than elasticity. This is because if this movement slowed down as it is shared among more matter while moving away from the source of light, it could not preserve such great speed over vast distances. # OF LIGHT. CHAP. I.

...emit<note>The previous page ended mid-word; based on the context of transmitting movement, the full word was likely "transmettre" (to transmit or emit).</note> the light of the Sun and the Stars.

I have therefore shown in what way one can conceive that light spreads successively by spherical waves, and how it is possible that this spreading occurs with such great speed as experiments and celestial observations require. Here it must also be noted that although the particles of the ether are supposed to be in continual motion (for there are many reasons for this), the successive propagation of the waves cannot be hindered by it, because it does not consist in the transport of these particles, but only in a small disturbance <term>ébranlement (a physical shaking or vibration)</term>, which they cannot help communicating to those surrounding them, despite all the motion that agitates them and makes them change places among themselves.

<image-desc>A diagram showing a candle flame with three points labeled A, B, and C inside the flame. From each of these points, a series of concentric circles expands outwards towards the right, representing waves of light originating from different points within the luminous body.</image-desc>

But we must consider even more particularly the origin of these waves, and the manner in which they spread. And first, it follows from what has been said about the production of light, that every small spot of a luminous body, such as the Sun, a candle, or a glowing coal, generates its own waves, of which that spot is the center. Thus, in the flame of a candle, having distinguished the points **A**, **B**, and **C**; the concentric circles described around each of these points represent the waves that originate from them. And one must conceive of the same thing around every point of the surface, and even parts of the interior of this flame.

But as the percussions <term>percussions (the rapid strikes or pulses that create the wave)</term> at the center of these waves have no regular sequence, one should not imagine that the waves themselves follow each other at equal distances; and if these distances appear as such in this figure, it is rather to

<summary>Huygens explains that light travels as a "vibration" through moving ether particles rather than the particles themselves traveling. He introduces the idea that every individual point on a luminous object, like a candle flame, acts as an independent center for expanding spherical waves.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 15 of Christiaan Huygens' "Treatise on Light," where he began explaining how light travels as waves through a medium of tiny particles. ->T R E A T I S E<-

to mark the progress of a single wave in equal increments of time, as well as to represent several waves originating from the same center.

Furthermore, it should not seem inconceivable that such a prodigious quantity of waves can cross one another without confusion and without erasing each other. It is certain that a single particle of matter can serve several waves coming from different sides, or even from opposite directions; this is true not only if it is pushed by blows that follow closely one after another, but even by those that act upon it at the same instant. This occurs because the movement spreads successively. This can be proven by the row of equal spheres made of hard material mentioned previously. If one pushes similar spheres **A** and **D** at the same time from two opposite sides against this row, one will see each rebound with the same speed it had when approaching, while the entire row remains in its place; this happens even though the movement has passed through its entire length, and in two directions at once. And if these contrary movements happen to meet at the middle sphere **B**, or at another such as **C**, that sphere must compress and act as a spring <term>ressort (the elastic property of a material that allows it to store and release mechanical energy)</term> on both sides, and thus serve at the same instant to **transmit these two movements.**

<image-desc>A diagram illustrating the mechanical transmission of motion through elastic spheres. A sphere labeled 'A' is on the left, separated from a contiguous row of six spheres. Within this row, the fourth sphere from the left is labeled 'B' and the fifth is labeled 'C'. On the far right is a final sphere labeled 'D'.</image-desc>

But what may at first appear very strange, and even incredible, is that undulations <note>Huygens uses "undulations" to describe the wave-like spreading of light</note> produced by such small movements and such tiny corpuscles could extend to such immense distances—as, for example, from the sun or from the stars to us. For the force of these waves must weaken as they move away from their origin, so that the action of each one in particular will undoubtedly become incapable of being felt by our sight. But one will cease to be astonished when considering that at a great distance...

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER I.

...from the luminous body an infinity of waves, although originating from different points of this body, unite in such a way that they essentially compose only a single wave, which consequently must have enough strength to be felt. Thus, this infinite number of waves that arise at the same instant from all the points of a fixed star, which may be as large as the Sun, essentially form only a single wave, which is indeed strong enough to make an impression on our eyes. Furthermore, from each luminous point, several thousand waves can emerge in the shortest imaginable time, due to the frequent percussion <note>original: "percussion" — here meaning a strike or impact that initiates motion</note> of the corpuscles <term>corpuscules (the tiny, invisible particles that Huygens believed made up the "Ether")</term>, which strike the Ether <term>Ether (a theoretical, weightless substance once believed to fill all space and serve as the medium for light waves)</term> at these points; this further contributes to making their action more perceptible.

There is also to consider in the emanation <note>The process of spreading outward from a source</note> of these waves that each particle of the matter in which a wave spreads must not communicate its motion only to the next particle located in a straight line from the luminous point; it also necessarily transmits motion to all the others that touch it and oppose its movement.

continued to **D C F**; and it is clear that there will be only the spot *c* of the wave **K C L** which will touch the wave **D C F**, namely the one that is on the straight line drawn through **A B**. Likewise, the other particles contained within the sphere **D C F**, such as *b b, d d* etc., will each have made their own wave. But each of these waves can only be infinitely weak compared to the wave **D C F**, to the composition of which all the others contribute by the part of their surface that is furthest from the center **A**.

One sees, furthermore, that the wave **D C F** is determined by the extremity of the movement which has emerged from point **A** in a certain space of time; there being no movement beyond this wave, although there is indeed some within the space it encloses, namely in the parts of the individual waves, which parts do not touch the sphere **D C F**. And all this should not seem to be pursued with too much care, nor too much subtlety; since it will be seen in what follows that all the properties of light, and everything pertaining to its reflection and its refraction, are explained primarily by this means. This is what was not known to those who previously began to consider the waves of light, among whom are <term>Mr. Hook <note>Robert Hooke (1635–1703), an English scientist who proposed an early wave theory of light.</note></term> in his <term>Micrographia <note>A landmark book published in 1665 detailing Hooke's microscopic observations.</note></term>, and <term>Father Pardies <note>Ignace-Gaston Pardies (1636–1673), a French Jesuit whose work on optics influenced Huygens.</note></term>, who in a treatise of which he showed me a part, and which he could not finish having died shortly after, had undertaken to prove by these waves the effects of reflection and refraction. But the principal foundation, which consists of the observation I have just made, was missing from his demonstrations, and for the rest, he held opinions very different from mine, as perhaps one will see someday if his writing has been preserved.

To come to the properties of light; let us first remark that each part of a wave must spread out in such a way that its extremities are always contained between the same straight lines drawn from the luminous point. Thus the part of the wave

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' foundational argument for the wave theory of light. Having established how wavelets combine to form a wavefront, he now addresses the physical nature of the medium (the ether) and critiques the competing "pressure" theory proposed by René Descartes.</meta>

-> TREATISE <-

**and thus we can treat rays of light as if they were straight lines.**

It further appears, from what has been observed regarding the weakness of individual wavelets, that it is not strictly necessary for all the particles of the <term>Ether</term> <note>The "Ether" was a hypothetical substance that 17th-century scientists believed filled all of space to allow light to travel, similar to how air carries sound.</note> to be equal to one another, although equality is more conducive to the propagation of movement. For while it is true that inequality will cause a particle, when pushing a larger one, to exert an effort to recoil with a portion of its movement, this will only generate a few individual wavelets backward toward the luminous point. These are incapable of producing light, and do not form a collective wave composed of many others, as the wave CE did.

Another of the most marvelous properties of light is that, when it comes from different sides, or even from opposite directions, the rays perform their effect through one another without any hindrance. This is why several spectators can see different objects all at once through the same opening, and why two people can look into each other's eyes at the same instant. Now, according to what has been explained regarding the action of light—and how its waves do not destroy or interrupt one another when they cross—these effects I have just mentioned are easy to conceive. However, they are not at all easy to understand, in my opinion, according to the view of <term>Des-Cartes</term> <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), whose theory of light Huygens is challenging here.</note>, who claims that light consists of a continual <term>pressure</term> <note>Descartes argued light wasn't a moving wave, but an instantaneous "tendency to move" or pressure exerted through a medium.</note> that only tends toward movement. For since this pressure cannot act from two opposite sides at once against bodies that have no inclination to approach one another, it is impossible to understand what I have just said about two people seeing each other mutually, nor how two torches could illuminate one another.

## ON REFLECTION.

Having explained the effects of light waves, which spread through <term>homogeneous matter</term> <note>A substance that is uniform in composition and properties throughout.</note>, we will next examine what happens to them when they encounter other bodies. We shall first show how these same waves explain the **Reflection** of light, and why it maintains the equality of angles <note>The Law of Reflection, which states that the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.</note>. <meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration of how light reflects off a surface. On the previous page, Huygens established the wavefront AC approaching the surface AB. Point **c** of wave **AC**, in a certain span of time, will have moved forward to plane **AB** at point **B**, following the straight line **CB**, which we must imagine coming from the luminous center, and which is consequently perpendicular to **AC**. Now, in this same span of time, point **A** of the same wave—having been prevented from communicating its motion beyond the plane **AB**, or at least in part—must have continued its motion in the matter above this plane, and this across a distance equal to **CB**; creating its own <term>particular spherical wave</term> <note>Now commonly known as a "secondary wavelet," this is the core of Huygens' Principle: every point on a wave acts as a new source of light.</note>, following what was said above. This wave is represented here by the circumference **SNR**, where the center is **A**, and the radius **AN** is equal to **CB**.

If we then consider the other points **H** of the wave **AC**, it appears that they will not only have arrived at the surface **AB** via the straight lines **HK** parallel to **CB**, but also that they will have generated, from centers **K**, particular spherical waves within the <term>diaphanous medium</term> <note>A transparent substance, like air or glass, that allows light to pass through.</note>, represented here by circumferences whose radii are equal to **KM**, that is to say, to the continuations of **HK** as far as the straight line **BG** (which is parallel to **AC**).

But all these circumferences have as a common <term>tangent</term> <note>A straight line that touches the curve of the circular waves at a single point.</note> the straight line **BN**; namely, the same line which, from **B**, is made tangent to the first of these circles, of which **A** was the center and **AN** the radius equal to **BC**, as is easy to see.

It is therefore the line **BN** (contained between **B** and the point **N**, where the perpendicular from point **A** falls) which is, as it were, formed by all these circumferences, and which terminates the movement resulting from the reflection of wave **AC**; and it is also where this movement is found in much greater quantity than anywhere else. This is why, according to what has been explained, **BN** is the propagation of wave **AC** at the moment when its point **c** has arrived at **B**. For there is no other line which, like **BN**, is a common tangent to all the said circles, unless it be **BG**, below the plane **AB**; which **BG** would be the propagation of the wave if

I also stated in the previous demonstration that the movement from point **A** of the incident wave could not be communicated beyond the plane **AB**, or at least not entirely. It should be noted here that although the movement of the <term>ethereal matter</term> <note>The "aether" was a hypothetical substance that 17th-century scientists believed filled all space, serving as the medium through which light waves traveled, much like air carries sound.</note> might be partially communicated to the matter of the reflecting body, this cannot in any way alter the speed of the waves' progress, upon which the angle of reflection depends. For a light <term>percussion</term> <note>In this context, Huygens uses "percussion" to mean a physical impact or strike—the way one particle hits another to pass on energy.</note> must generate waves as fast as a very strong one, within the same material. This comes from the property of bodies that possess elasticity; of which we spoke earlier; namely, that whether they are compressed a little or a lot, they return to their shape in equal amounts of time. Therefore, in any reflection of light, against whatever body it may be, the angles of reflection and incidence must be equal; notwithstanding that the body might be of such a nature that it removes part of the movement that constitutes the incident light. And experience shows that indeed there is no polished body whose reflection does not follow this rule.

But what must be noted above all in our demonstration is that it does not require the reflecting surface to be considered as a smooth plane; as all those who have tried to explain the effects of reflection have assumed; but only of a uniformity such as can be composed by the particles of the matter of the reflecting body, placed side by side; these particles are larger than those of the ethereal matter, as will appear from what we shall say when treating the transparency and opacity of bodies. For with the surface thus consisting of particles grouped together, and the ethereal particles being on top and smaller, it is evident that one cannot demonstrate the equality of the angles of incidence,

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 25. Huygens is contrasting the traditional particle-based explanation of light (comparing light to a bouncing ball) with his new wave theory. He is specifically addressing why even microscopically "rough" surfaces like mercury still reflect light as if they were perfectly smooth planes.</meta>

->T R E A T I S E<-

and of reflection by the resemblance to what happens to a ball struck against a wall—an explanation that has always been used. In contrast, in our approach, the matter is explained without difficulty. For the smallness of the particles of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "vif argent"; commonly known as mercury</note>, for example, is such that one must imagine millions of them within even the smallest visible surface, arranged like a pile of grains of sand that has been smoothed as much as possible. This surface then becomes as even as a polished glass to our eyes; and although it remains rough in relation to the particles of the <term>Ether</term> <note>The theoretical medium that Huygens believed carried light waves, similar to how air carries sound waves.</note>, it is evident that the centers of all the individual spheres of reflection, which we have discussed, lie roughly on the same flat plane. Thus, a common tangent can fit them perfectly enough for what is required to produce light. This is the only thing required in our method of demonstration to ensure the equality of the said angles, without the rest of the movement reflected in all directions being able to produce any contrary effect.

## CHAPTER III.

### ON REFRACTION.

**Just** as the effects of Reflection have been explained by light waves reflecting off the surface of polished bodies, we shall explain transparency and the phenomena of refraction by waves that extend into and through <term>diaphanous bodies</term> <note>Transparent materials that allow light to pass through.</note>, whether solid, like glass, or liquid, like water, oils, etc. But so that it does not seem strange to suppose this passage of waves inside these bodies, I will first show that it can be conceived as possible in more than one way.

First, then, if the ethereal matter did not penetrate

<meta>This page continues Huygens's explanation of how light travels through different mediums. On the previous page, he began explaining that refraction occurs because light waves move through "diaphanous" (transparent) bodies. Here, he addresses the physical composition of these bodies to explain how they transmit waves.</meta>

<header>ON LIGHT. CHAP. III.</header>

...transparent bodies in any way, their own particles could successively communicate the movement of waves to one another, just like those of the <term>Ether</term> <note>The invisible, subtle medium that Huygens believed filled all space and carried light waves, similar to how air carries sound.</note>; being assumed, like the latter, to be of an elastic nature <note>original: "nature à faire reſſort"; literally "of a nature to make a spring," meaning the particles can compress and bounce back, which is essential for wave propagation.</note>. And this is easy to conceive regarding water and other transparent liquids, as they are composed of detached particles. But it may seem more difficult regarding glass and other hard, transparent bodies, because their solidity does not seem to allow them to receive movement except in their entire mass at once. This, however, is not necessary, because this solidity is not as it appears to us; it is probable that these bodies are rather composed of particles that are merely placed next to one another, and held together by some external pressure from another matter, and by the irregularity of their shapes. For firstly, their <term>rarity</term> <note>In this context, rarity refers to the quality of being porous or having gaps between particles, rather than being a single, solid block.</note> is apparent from the ease with which the matter of <term>magnetic vortices</term> <note>original: "tourbillons de l'aimant"; referring to the then-common Cartesian theory that magnets work by swirling "vortices" of invisible particles.</note> passes through them, as does the matter that causes <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "peſanteur"; the force that gives objects weight and pulls them toward the Earth.</note>. Furthermore, one cannot say that these bodies have a texture like that of a sponge or light bread, because the heat of fire makes them flow, and thereby changes the arrangement of the particles among themselves. It remains, then, as has been said, that they are assemblies of particles that touch one another without composing a continuous solid. This being the case, the movement that these particles receive to continue the waves of light—only communicating it from one to another without them leaving their place or disturbing one another—can very well take its effect without in any way harming the solidity of the compound as it appears to us.

Par the "external pressure" of which I spoke, one must not understand that of the air, which would not be sufficient, but another from a more subtle matter, which pressure manifests itself in that experiment which chance led me to encounter a long—

<sig>D 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: time;</meta>

<summary>Huygens argues that transparent solids like glass are not truly solid but are composed of individual particles held together by external pressure, allowing light waves to pass through them by vibrating the particles in place.</summary>
<keywords>wave theory, elasticity, transparent bodies, magnetism, gravity, atomic structure, ether, refraction</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Huygens's argument regarding the composition of transparent materials. On the previous page, he began discussing how the particles of solid bodies might transmit light waves, suggesting that even solid glass is porous and held together by external pressure.</meta>

<header>T R E A T I S E</header>

time; namely, water purged of air, which remains suspended in a glass tube open at the bottom, despite the air being removed from the vessel in which this tube is enclosed.

One can, in this manner, conceive of transparency without the need for the <term>ethereal matter</term> <note>Huygens's term for the "ether," an invisible, fluid-like substance he believed filled all space and carried light waves, much like air carries sound.</note>—which serves to carry light—to pass through it, or for it to find pores to slip into. But the truth is that this matter not only passes through, but does so with great ease, of which the experiment of <term>Torricelli</term> <note>Evangelista Torricelli (1608–1647), the Italian physicist who invented the barometer. His experiment showed that a vacuum (the "Torricellian vacuum") is created when mercury falls in a sealed tube.</note>, cited above, is already a proof. Because as the <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "vif argent"; the historical name for the liquid metal mercury.</note> and water leave the upper part of the glass tube, it appears that this space is immediately filled with ethereal matter, since light still passes through it. But here is another argument that proves this easy penetrability, not only in transparent bodies, but also in all others.

When light passes through a hollow glass sphere, sealed on all sides, it is certain that it is as full of ethereal matter as the spaces outside the sphere. And this ethereal matter, as has been shown previously, consists of particles that touch each other very closely. If it were so enclosed within the sphere that it could not escape through the pores of the glass, it would be forced to follow the movement of the sphere when its position is changed. Consequently, roughly the same force would be required to impart a certain speed to this sphere, when placed on a horizontal plane, as if it were full of water or perhaps quicksilver. This is because every body resists the speed of the motion one tries to give it, according to the quantity of matter it contains and which must follow that motion <note>Huygens is describing the principle of inertia: that the "resistance" to movement (mass) depends on the total amount of matter present.</note>. But on the contrary, we find that the sphere only resists the impulse of movement according to the amount of glass matter from which it is made. Therefore, the ethereal matter within must not be trapped, but must flow through the glass with very great freedom.

<summary>Huygens argues that the "ethereal matter" responsible for light must be able to flow freely through solid objects. He proves this by noting that a hollow glass sphere does not gain extra "resistance to movement" (inertia) from the ether inside it, which would happen if the ether were trapped.</summary>

# ON LIGHT. CHAP. III.

<sig>D 3</sig>

We shall show hereafter that the same penetrability is also concluded, by this means, in the case of **opaque bodies**.

The second way, then, of explaining **transparence**, and which appears more likely, is by saying that the waves of light continue within the <term>ethereal matter</term> <note>Huygens’s term for the "ether," the invisible, fluid-like substance he believed filled all space and carried light waves, much like air carries sound.</note>, which continually occupies the *interstices*, or pores of transparent bodies. For since it passes through them continually, and with ease, it follows that they are always filled with it. And one can even demonstrate that these interstices occupy much more space than the coherent particles that constitute the bodies. For if what we have just said is true—that force is required to impart a certain horizontal velocity to bodies in proportion to the amount of coherent matter they contain; and if the proportion of this force follows the ratio of weights, which is confirmed by experience; then the quantity of the constituent matter of bodies also follows the proportion of weights. Now we see that water weighs only the fourteenth part as much as an equal portion of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "vif argent"; the historical name for the liquid metal mercury.</note>: therefore the matter of water does not occupy even the fourteenth part of the space that its mass takes up. It must even occupy much less, since quicksilver is less heavy than gold; and the matter of gold is very little dense: as follows from the fact that the matter of the <term>vortices of the magnet</term> <note>Huygens refers to a theory popular at the time—championed by René Descartes—that magnetism was caused by swirling "vortices" of invisible particles flowing through materials.</note>, and that which causes gravity, pass through it very freely.

But it may be objected here that, if the body of water is of such great rarity, and that its particles occupy such a small portion of the space of its apparent extent, it is very strange how it nevertheless resists **Compression** so strongly, without allowing itself to be condensed by any force that has been tried thus far; even preserving all its **liquidity**, while it suffers this pressure.

This is no small difficulty. Which, however, one can

<summary>Huygens argues that transparent materials are mostly empty space filled with ether, using the relative weights of water and mercury to prove that "solid" matter occupies very little volume. He begins to address why water remains incompressible despite being so porous.</summary>

<note>Treatise</note>

...can be resolved by saying that the very violent and rapid movement of the <term>subtle matter</term> <note>A term used in 17th-century physics to describe an invisible substance made of extremely fine, fast-moving particles that fills the voids in larger matter.</note> which makes water liquid, by agitating the particles of which it is composed, maintains this liquidity despite any pressure that has been thought to be applied to it until now.

The thinness of transparent bodies being thus as we have stated, one easily conceives that the waves can be continued within the ethereal matter which fills the gaps between the particles. Furthermore, one may believe that the progress of these waves must be a little slower inside these bodies, because of the small detours caused by these same particles. In this different speed of light, I shall show that the cause of **refraction** <note>The bending of light as it passes from one medium, like air, into another, like glass or water.</note> consists.

I shall first indicate the third and last way in which transparency may be conceived, which is by supposing that the movement of the waves of light is transmitted indifferently both through the particles of the ethereal matter, which occupy the gaps in bodies, and through the particles that compose them, so that this movement passes from one to the other. It will be seen hereafter that this hypothesis is very useful for explaining the **double refraction** <note>An optical phenomenon where a single ray of light entering certain crystals is split into two distinct rays.</note> of certain <term>diaphanous</term> <note>A historical term for transparent or clear substances.</note> bodies.

If one objects that the particles of the ether—being smaller than those of transparent bodies, since they pass through their intervals—it would follow that they could communicate only a little of their movement to them; one may answer that the particles of these bodies are themselves composed of other even smaller particles; and thus it will be these secondary particles that receive the movement from those of the ether.

Moreover, if those of transparent bodies have their <term>elasticity</term> <note>original: "ressort"; literally "spring." Huygens imagined matter as being made of tiny spheres that could bounce or transmit motion like springs.</note> a little less prompt than that of the ethereal particles—which nothing prevents us from supposing—it will follow again that the progress of the waves of light will be slower inside these bodies than it is outside in the ethereal matter.

### ->ON LIGHT. CHAPTER III.<-

That is all I have found most plausible regarding the way the waves of light pass through transparent bodies. To this, we must also add how these bodies differ from those that are opaque; all the more so because it might seem—given the easy penetration of bodies by the <term>ethereal matter</term> <note>Huygens refers to a weightless, invisible substance that was believed to fill all space and allow light to travel as waves, much like air carries sound.</note> of which we have spoken—that there would be no body that was not transparent. For by the same reasoning of the hollow sphere, which I used to prove the low density of glass and its easy penetrability by the ethereal matter, one can also prove that the same penetrability applies to metals and every other kind of body. 

For if this sphere were made of silver, for example, it is certain that it contains the ethereal matter that serves light, since this matter was present within it, as was the air, when the opening of the sphere was sealed. However, once closed and placed on a horizontal plane, it resists the movement one tries to give it only in proportion to the amount of silver from which it is made. Thus, one must conclude, as before, that the enclosed ethereal matter does not follow the movement of the sphere; and that, therefore, silver—just as well as glass—is very easily penetrated by this matter. This matter is therefore found continually and in abundance between the particles of silver and all other opaque bodies; and since it facilitates the propagation of light, it seems that these bodies should also be transparent, like glass; which, however, is not the case.

From where, then, shall we say their opacity arises? Is it because the particles that compose them are soft—that is to say, these particles, being composed of other smaller ones, are capable of changing shape upon receiving the impact of the ethereal particles, thereby dampening their movement and thus preventing the continuation of the light waves? <note>Huygens is exploring a "mechanical" explanation: if atoms are "soft" or "squishy," they might absorb the energy of a light wave like a pillow, whereas "hard" or elastic atoms would pass the vibration along.</note> This cannot be; for if the particles of metals were soft, how

What I find most plausible in this matter is to say that the bodies of metals, which are almost the only truly opaque substances, have soft particles interspersed among their hard ones; so that the former serve to cause reflection, and the latter to prevent transparency. In contrast, transparent bodies contain only hard particles which have the ability to act as a <term>spring</term> <note>Huygens uses the word "ressort" (spring) to describe elasticity, which allows the particles to transmit wave energy without absorbing it.</note> and serve, together with those of the <term>ethereal matter</term>, as has been said, for the propagation of light waves.

It is therefore **BN**—which is formed, as it were, by the small arcs of these circumferences—that terminates the movement that wave **AC** has communicated into the transparent body, and where this movement is found in much greater quantity than anywhere else. For this reason, this line (as has been stated more than once) is the propagation of the wave **AC** at the moment that its point **C** has arrived at **B**. # ON LIGHT. CHAPTER III.

One also sees the reason for a notable occurrence that happens in this refraction; which is that from a certain obliquity of the incident ray **D A**, it begins to be unable to penetrate into the other <term>transparent medium<gloss>the "diaphane" or substance light is traveling through</gloss></term>. For if the angle **D A Q** or **C B A** is such that in the triangle **A C B**, **C B** is equal to 2/3 of **A B**, or greater, then **A N** cannot form a side of the triangle **A N B** because it becomes equal to **A B**, or greater: so that the <term>wave front<gloss>Huygens's "partie d'onde"</gloss></term> **B N** is found nowhere, nor consequently **A N**, which ought to be perpendicular to it. And thus the incident ray **D A** does not then pierce the surface **A B**. <note>Huygens is demonstrating geometrically why light cannot pass from a denser medium to a lighter one at certain angles. If the math of the wave speeds requires the new wave front to be longer than the available surface, the light cannot "refract" out and must stay within the first medium.</note>

When the ratio of the speeds of the waves is two to three, as in our example, which is the one that corresponds to glass and air, the angle **D A Q** must be greater than 48 degrees 11 minutes in order for the ray **D A** to be able to pass through by refracting. And when the ratio of these speeds is 3 to 4, as it is very nearly in water and air, this angle **D A Q** must exceed 41 degrees 24 minutes. And this agrees perfectly with experience.

But one might ask here, since the meeting of wave **A C** against the surface **A B** must produce motion in the matter on the other side, why no light passes through there. To which the answer is easy if one remembers what has been said previously. For although an infinity of particular waves are generated in the matter on the other side of **A B**, it does not happen that these waves have a common tangent line (whether straight or curved) at one and the same instant; and thus there is no line that terminates the propagation of wave **A C** beyond the plane **A B**, nor where the movement is gathered in a large enough quantity to produce light. <note>In Huygens's theory, light is only "visible" where individual wavelets reinforce each other to form a coherent wave front. At these steep angles, the wavelets don't line up, so no light is perceived.</note> And one will easily see the truth of this—namely that **C B** being greater than 2/3 of **A B**, the waves excited beyond the plane **A B** will have no common tangent—if from the centers <unclear>x</unclear> one de-

He argues that light fails to pass through not because motion stops, but because the resulting wavelets do not form a coherent wave front.</summary>

Now it should be noted that as soon as the angle **D A Q** is smaller than what is necessary to allow the <term>refracted ray<gloss>le rayon rompu</gloss></term> **D A** to pass into the other <term>transparent medium<gloss>diaphane</gloss></term>, we find that the internal reflection, occurring at the surface **A B**, increases greatly in brightness; as is easy to experience with a triangular prism: for which we can provide this reason through our Theory. When the angle **D A Q** is still large enough to allow the ray **D A** to pass through, it is manifest that the light of the <term>wave front<gloss>partie d'onde</gloss></term> **A C** is gathered into a smaller extent when it has reached **B N**. It also appears that the wave **B N** becomes smaller as the angle **C B A** or **D A Q** is made smaller; until, having decreased to the limit previously noted, this wave **B N** is gathered entirely as if into a point. <note>Huygens is describing the "critical angle." At this point, the wave front in the second medium disappears because the geometry no longer allows for a new wave front to form outside the surface.</note> That is to say, when the location **C** of the wave **A C** has then arrived at **B**, the wave **B N**, which is the propagation of **A C**, is entirely reduced to the same point **B**; just as, when the location **H** had arrived at **K**, the part **A H** was entirely reduced to the same point **K**. This shows that as the wave **C A** has come to meet the surface **A B**, a great quantity of motion was found along this surface; which motion must also have spread inside the transparent body, and have greatly reinforced the individual waves that produce internal reflection against the surface **A B**, following the laws of reflection previously explained. <note>In modern terms, Huygens is arguing for the conservation of energy: if the light energy cannot pass through the surface, it must be reflected back, which is why total internal reflection is so much brighter than ordinary partial reflection.</note>

### ON LIGHT. CHAP. III.

...is reduced to nothing, and thus the wave **B N** is reduced to a point. From this it follows that the internal reflection, which was obscure, suddenly becomes bright as soon as the angle of incidence is such that it no longer allows passage for refraction. <note>Huygens is explaining why a surface suddenly becomes like a mirror when viewed from a certain angle—this is what we now call Total Internal Reflection.</note>

Now, as for ordinary external reflection—that is to say, that which occurs when the angle of incidence **D A Q** is still large enough to allow the <term>refracted ray<gloss>le rayon rompu</gloss></term> to penetrate beyond the surface **A B**—this reflection must occur against the particles of matter that touch the transparent body from the outside. It is apparently against the particles of the air and others, mixed among the <term>ethereal matter<gloss>matière éthérée</gloss></term>, and coarser than it. <note>Huygens believed light traveled through an "ether," a fine substance filling all space. Here he suggests that reflection is caused by light bumping into larger particles of air or the object itself.</note> Just as, on the other hand, the external reflection of these bodies occurs against the particles that compose them, which are also larger than those of the ethereal matter, since the latter flows through their intervals. It is true that some difficulty remains in this regarding experiments where this internal reflection occurs without the particles of air being able to contribute, as in vessels or tubes from <unclear>which</unclear> the air has been removed.

Moreover, experience teaches us that these two reflections are of nearly equal strength, and that in different transparent bodies, they are stronger as the refraction of those bodies is greater. Thus, one sees clearly that the reflection of glass is stronger than that of water, and that of diamond stronger than that of glass.

I will conclude this theory of refraction by demonstrating a remarkable proposition that depends on it: namely, that a ray of light, in order to go from one point to another when these points are in different <term>transparent media<gloss>diaphanes</gloss></term>, refracts at the flat surface joining these two media in such a way that it employs the **least possible time**; just as happens in reflection against a flat surface. Mr. *Fermat* was the first to propose this property of refractions, holding, as we do, and dire-
