### ON LIGHT. CHAP. III.

A B, F O parallel to A B; B H perpendicular to F O, and F G on B C.

Since therefore the angle H B F is equal to P B A, and the angle B F G equal to Q B C, it follows that the <term>sine<note>A trigonometric function representing the ratio of sides in a right-angled triangle; here used to define the angle of the light ray.</note></term> of angle H B F will also have to the sine of B F G the same ratio as the speed of light in <term>transparent medium<gloss>diaphane</gloss></term> A has to its speed in transparent medium C. But these sines are the straight lines H F, B G, taking B F for the radius <gloss>semi-diameter</gloss> of a circle. Therefore these lines, H F, B G, have between them the said ratio of speeds. And consequently, the time taken by light through H F, assuming the ray was O F, would be equal to the time through B G within the transparent medium C. But the time through A B is equal to the time through O H; therefore the time through O F is equal to the time through A B, B G. Furthermore, the time through F C is longer than through G C; therefore the time through O F C will be longer than through A B C. But A F is greater than O F; therefore the time through A F C will exceed the time through A B C even more.

Now let us assume that the ray has come from A to C through A K, K C; the point of <term>refraction<note>The bending of light as it passes from one medium (like air) into another (like water or glass).</note></term> K being closer to A than point B is; and let C N be perpendicular to B C, K N parallel to B C; B M perpendicular to K N, and K L to B A.

Here B L and K M are the sines of the angles B K L, K B M, that is to say, of the angles P B A, Q B C; and consequently they are to each other as the speed of light in transparent medium A is to the speed in transparent medium C. Therefore the time through L B is equal to the time through K M; and since the time through B C is equal to the time through M N, the time through L B C will be equal to the time through K M N. But the time through A K is longer than through A L; therefore the time through A K N is longer than through A B C. And K C being longer than K N, the time through A K C will exceed the time through A B C even more. Thus it appears that the time through A B C is the shortest it can be: which was to be demonstrated <note>original: "ce qu’il falloit demonſtrer"—this is the standard concluding phrase for a mathematical proof, often abbreviated as Q.E.D.</note>.

## CHAPTER IV.

### ON THE REFRACTION OF THE AIR.

**W**e have shown how the movement that creates light spreads through spherical waves in a <term>homogeneous matter<note>A substance that has the same properties and composition throughout; in this context, Huygens is referring to the "aether" through which light was thought to travel.</note></term>. And it is evident that when the matter is not homogeneous, but of such a constitution that the movement communicates more quickly toward one side than another, these waves cannot be spherical; instead, they must take their shape according to the different spaces that the successive movement travels in equal amounts of time.

It is by this principle that we will first explain the <term>refractions<note>The bending of light as it passes through layers of air of different densities.</note></term> that occur in the air, which extends from here to the clouds and beyond. The effects of these refractions are quite remarkable; for it is because of them that we often see objects that the curvature of the Earth would otherwise hide from us, such as islands and the summits of mountains when one is at sea. Because of them, the Sun and the Moon also appear to have risen before they actually have, and appear to set later; so much so that one has often seen the Moon eclipsed while the Sun still appeared above the horizon. And thus the heights of the Sun and the Moon, and those of all the stars, always appear a little higher—due to these same refractions—than they truly are, as astronomers well know.

But there is an experiment that makes this refraction very visible: by fixing a <term>telescope<note>original: "lunette d’approche," literally a "nearing glass."</note></term> in a certain spot so that it points at an object half a league or more away, such as a steeple or a house, if one looks at different hours—

<meta>catchword: —tes (completing the word "differentes" or "different")</meta>

<summary>Huygens explains how light waves lose their spherical shape when traveling through non-uniform mediums like the atmosphere, leading to optical phenomena like seeing objects below the horizon or the early appearance of the Sun.</summary>

<keywords>atmospheric refraction, light waves, homogeneous medium, astronomy, telescope, sunrise, sunset, optical illusions</keywords>

...times of the day, leaving it always fixed in the same way, one will see that it will not be the same parts of the object that appear in the middle of the telescope's opening; rather, typically in the morning and evening, when there are more <term>vapors<note>In this context, moisture or mist in the air that changes its density.</note></term> near the Earth, these objects seem to rise higher, so that half or more of them will no longer be visible; and they will lower toward midday when these vapors have dissipated.

Those who only consider <term>refraction<note>The bending of light as it passes through different substances.</note></term> as occurring at the surfaces that separate transparent bodies of different natures would have difficulty accounting for everything I have just reported; but following our Theory the matter is very easy. It is known that the air surrounding us, besides the particles proper to it which swim in the <term>ethereal matter<note>original: "matiere etherée." Scientists of this era believed a weightless fluid called "aether" filled the universe and carried light waves, much like air carries sound.</note></term>, as has been explained, also fills with water particles which the action of heat raises; and it has been recognized elsewhere by very certain experiments that the density of the air diminishes as one ascends higher. Now, whether the particles of water and those of air participate—by means of the particles of ethereal matter—in the movement that creates light, but possess a less prompt <term>elasticity<note>original: "ressort," literally "spring." Huygens refers to the speed at which a medium can bounce back and transmit a wave.</note></term> than the ether; or whether the collision and the obstruction that these parts of air and water offer to the propagation of the movement of the ethereal particles delays its progress; it follows that both, drifting among the ethereal particles, must make the air—from a great height down to the Earth—gradually less conducive to the expansion of the <term>waves of light<note>Huygens was the primary architect of the wave theory of light, proposing that light spreads like ripples in a pond.</note></term>.

From this, the shape of the waves must become approximately what this figure represents. Namely, if A is a light source, or the visible point of a steeple, the waves that originate from it must—

And this is the very reason why, at certain times, a **distant object** will be hidden behind another less distant one, and yet it can be seen at another time, even though the spot from which one looks remains always the same. But the reason for this effect will be even more evident by what we are about to remark concerning the **curvature of rays**. It appears from the things explained above that the progress, or the **propagation** of a particle of a **light wave**, is properly what we call a **ray**. Now, while these rays are straight in **homogeneous diaphanous** <note>Transparent materials that are uniform in density throughout.</note> materials, they must be curved in air of unequal **penetrability** <note>Huygens uses "penetrability" to describe how easily light passes through a medium; in modern terms, we would relate this to the refractive index.</note>. For they necessarily follow the line which, from the **object** to the eye, cuts all the progressions of the waves at right angles, just as the **line A E B** does in the first figure, as will be shown hereafter; and it is this line that determines which interposed bodies should prevent us from seeing the object or not. For although the point of the **steeple A** appears raised at **D**, it would nevertheless not appear to the **eye B** if the **tower H** were between them, because it crosses the curve **A E B**. But the **tower E**, which is below this curve, does not at all prevent the point **A** from being seen. Now, as the air near the **Earth** exceeds in **density** that which is higher up, the **curvature of the ray A E B** becomes greater, so that at certain times it passes above the summit **E**, which allows the point **A** to be perceived by the eye at **B**; and at other times it is interrupted by the same **tower E**, which hides **A** from that same eye.

if we draw the straight line **KL** which touches these two circles, it is

<summary>Huygens uses a geometric proof to show how light wavefronts change as they move through atmospheric layers of varying density. He demonstrates that because light travels faster in the higher, thinner air than in the denser air near the Earth, the resulting wavefront becomes tilted.</summary>

of the **particular waves** <term>particular waves</term> <note>These are "wavelets," the secondary waves that spread from every point on a primary wavefront, a core part of Huygens' Wave Theory.</note> born from the points of the wave **AB**, and this movement will be strongest between the points **K** and **L** than anywhere else at that same instant, since an infinite number of circumferences concur to form this straight line. Consequently, **KL** will be the propagation of the wave part **AB**, following what was said when explaining ordinary reflection and refraction. Now it appears that **AK** and **BL** incline toward the side where the air is less easy to penetrate <note>Meaning the side where the air is denser, usually closer to the ground.</note>: for **AK** being longer than **BL**, and being parallel to it, it follows that if the lines **AB** and **KL** were extended, they would meet on the side of **L**. But the angle **K** is a right angle, therefore **KAB** is necessarily acute, and thus smaller than **DAB**. If one looks in the same manner for the progress of the wave part **KL**, one will find that in another period of time it has reached **MN**, such that the perpendiculars **KM** and **LN** incline even more than **AK** and **BL**. And this sufficiently demonstrates that the ray continues along the curved line that cuts all the waves at right angles, as has been stated.

# CHAPTER V.

## OF THE STRANGE REFRACTION OF ICELAND CRYSTAL.

I. There is brought from **Iceland**, which is an Island in the Northern Sea at a latitude of 66 degrees, a species of **Crystal**, or transparent stone, very remarkable for its shape and other qualities, but above all for its strange refractions. The causes of these seemed to me all the more worthy of being carefully investigated because, among all transparent bodies, this one alone does not follow the ordinary rules regarding light rays. I even found it necessary to conduct this research because the refractions of this Crystal seemed to overturn our previous explanation of regular

singular; which, on the contrary, one will see that they confirm <note>The "they" refers to the strange properties of the crystal.</note> greatly, after being reduced to the same principle. It is in **Iceland** that one finds large pieces of this **Crystal**, of which I have seen some weighing 4 or 5 pounds. But it also grows in other countries: for I have had some of the same species that was found in **France** near the city of **Troyes** in **Champagne**, and another that came from the **Isle of Corsica**, although both were less clear, and only in small pieces, barely capable of making any effect of refraction noticeable.

2. The first knowledge that the public had of it is due to **Mr. Erasmus Bartholin** <note>Erasmus Bartholin (1625–1698) was a Danish scientist who first published a description of double refraction in 1669.</note>, who gave the description of **Iceland crystal** along with that of its principal phenomena. But I will not fail to give my own here, both for the instruction of those who will not have seen his book, and because in some of these phenomena there is a little difference between his observations and those I have made: having applied myself with great exactness to examine these properties of **refraction**, in order to be quite sure of them before undertaking to clarify the causes.

3. If one looks at the hardness of this stone, and the quality it has of being easily split, it must rather be esteemed a species of **Talc** <term>Talc</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Talc" was a broader category for soft, flaky, or translucent minerals.</note>, rather than **Crystal**. For an iron point marks it as easily as other Talc, or as **Alabaster**, which it equals in weight.

4. Even so, if one wishes, all six faces may be equal and similar **rhombi**. The figure added here represents a piece of this **Crystal**. I have also experimented that it can be made red hot in a fire without being altered in any way, nor rendered less **diaphanous** <term>diaphanous</term> <note>Transparent or translucent.</note>; but a very violent fire will nevertheless **calcine** <term>calcine</term> <note>To heat a substance to a high temperature, but below its melting point, to bring about thermal decomposition or remove volatile fractions.</note> it. Its transparency is hardly less than that of water or **rock crystal**, and it is without any color. But the rays of light pass through it in another fashion, and produce these marvelous **refractions**, the causes of which I will now attempt to explain; leaving until the end of this **Treatise** my conjectures regarding the formation and the extraordinary figure of this **Crystal**.

**12.** The way to perform these observations accurately is as follows. One must trace on a sheet of paper, fixed to a very smooth table, a black line **A B**, and two others that intersect it at right angles, **C E D** and **K M L**, placed more or less far apart depending on whether one wishes to examine a more or less oblique ray. Then, by placing the eye directly above the line **A B**, it will appear only as a single line, and one will see that the part seen through the crystal, along with the parts appearing outside, will meet in a straight line. However, the line **C D** will appear double, and one will distinguish the image coming from the **regular refraction** <term>regular refraction</term> <note>Another term for the "ordinary" refraction; the image that behaves as light does in normal glass.</note> by the fact that it appears higher than the other when looking with both eyes, or else because by turning the crystal on the paper, it remains steady, while the other image moves and rotates all around.

One should then place the eye at **I** (remaining always in the perpendicular plane passing through **A B**) so that it sees the image of the line **C D**, which comes from the **regular refraction**, forming a straight line with the rest of that line which is outside the crystal. And by marking then on the surface of the crystal the point **H**, where the intersection **E** appears, this point will be directly above **E**. Then one should move the eye back toward **O**, still in the perpendicular plane...

### ON LIGHT. CHAP. V.

->H 2<-

...certain and invariable. Concerning these particles, and their form and arrangement, I will propose my conjectures at the end of this Treatise, along with some experiments that confirm them.

20. The double emanation of light waves that I had imagined became more probable to me after a certain phenomenon I observed in ordinary crystal <note>Huygens is referring to rock crystal or quartz.</note> that grows in a hexagonal shape, and which, because of this regularity, also seems to be composed of particles of a certain shape and arranged in order. This was because this crystal has a <term>double refraction</term><note>The phenomenon where a single ray of light entering a material is split into two different rays, creating a double image.</note>, just like that of Iceland <note>A reference to "Iceland spar," a transparent calcite known for its high birefringence.</note>, although less obvious. For having had well-polished **Prisms** cut from it through different sections, I noticed in all of them—when looking at a candle flame through them, or at the lead of the window panes—that everything appeared double, though the images were not very far apart. From this, I understood the reason why this body, though so transparent, is useless for <term>telescopes</term><note>original: "Lunettes d'approche"</note> when they are of any significant length.

21. Now this double refraction, according to my theory established above, seemed to require a double emanation of light waves, both spherical (since both refractions are regular) and with one simply being a bit slower than the other. For by this, the phenomenon is explained quite naturally, by assuming the substances that serve as a medium for these waves, just as I did for the Iceland crystal. I therefore had less difficulty after that in admitting two emanations of waves within the same body. And because one might object to me that, by composing these two crystals of equal particles of a certain shape and stacked regularly, the gaps that these particles leave—and which contain the <term>ethereal matter</term><note>The "aether," a hypothetical substance once believed to fill all space and act as the medium for light waves.</note>—would hardly be enough to transmit the light waves I have placed there; I removed this difficulty by considering these particles as having a very <term>loose texture</term><note>original: "tissu fort rare"; implying the particles are not solid blocks but are porous or sparse in composition.</note>, or rather being composed of...

<summary>Huygens explains that ordinary quartz also exhibits double refraction, which supports his theory of light traveling in multiple waves. He notes that this property makes quartz unsuitable for long telescopes and addresses how light moves through the "ethereal matter" between crystal particles.</summary>
<keywords>double refraction, light waves, quartz, Iceland spar, telescopes, ethereal matter, optics, crystal structure</keywords>

...of other much smaller particles, between which the **ethereal matter** <note>The "aether" was a hypothesized invisible medium that early scientists believed filled all space and transmitted light waves, much like air transmits sound.</note> passes very freely. This, moreover, necessarily follows from what was demonstrated earlier regarding the small amount of solid matter of which bodies are assembled.

22. Supposing then these spheroidal waves in addition to the spherical ones, I began to examine whether they could serve to explain the phenomena of <term>irregular refraction</term><note>Also known as birefringence; the phenomenon where a single ray of light entering a crystal splits into two rays that travel in different directions.</note>, and how through these same phenomena I could determine the shape and the position of the spheroids. In this, I finally obtained the desired success, by proceeding as follows.

23. I first considered the effect of the waves thus formed with respect to the ray that falls perpendicularly upon the flat surface of a transparent body, in which they would extend in this manner.

<image-desc>A geometric scientific diagram illustrating wave theory. A horizontal line AB represents a surface. Above it, a parallel line RC represents an incoming wave front. Below surface AB, several semi-elliptical (spheroidal) wave fronts are shown originating from the points of incidence (A, K, k, B). I set **AB** as the exposed part of the surface. And since a ray perpendicular to a plane, and coming from a very distant light source, is nothing other—according to the preceding Theory—than the <term>incidence</term> <note>The arrival or "striking" of light upon a surface.</note> of a small portion of a wave parallel to that plane; I supposed the straight line **RC**, parallel and equal to **AB**, to be a portion of a wave of light, whose infinite points **R, H, h, C** come to meet the surface **AB** at the points **A, K, k, B**. Therefore, instead of the individual hemispherical waves which, in a body with ordinary refraction, would spread from each of these latter points (as we have explained above when treating refraction); these here must be <term>hemi-spheroids</term><note>Half of a spheroid, which is a sphere that has been stretched or flattened like an oval or a grape.</note>. I supposed that the axes, or rather the major diameters, of these were obli- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "oblique" to be continued on the next page.</note>

24. Now, it appeared that this common tangent **N Q** was parallel to **A B**, and of the same length, but that it was not directly opposite it. And it is in this way that I understood what had previously seemed very difficult to me: how a ray perpendicular to a surface could suffer **refraction** <note>Usually, light only bends if it hits a surface at an angle. Furthermore, this diversity of proportions of refraction produces a very singular effect in this Crystal, which is that by placing it upon a paper, where there are letters or other things marked; if one looks down upon it, with both eyes situated in the plane of the section through *E F*, one sees the letters more elevated <note>Huygens is describing the optical illusion where an object beneath a crystal appears to be closer to the surface than it actually is due to refraction.</note> by this irregular refraction, than when one places the eyes in the plane of the section through *A H*; and the difference of the elevations appears by the other ordinary refraction of this crystal, whose proportion is as 5 to 3, and which always elevates these letters equally, and higher than the irregular refraction does. For one sees the letters, and the paper where they are written, as if in two different stories <note>original: "étages". Huygens uses the metaphor of building floors to describe the different depths at which the two images of the letters appear.</note> all at once; and in the first position of the eyes, namely when they are in the plane through *A H*, these two stories are four times further from each other than when the eyes are in the plane through *E F*.

Let a semicircle be described on *Qq*, which cuts the ray *CR* at *B*, from which let *BV* be drawn perpendicular to *Qq*; and let the proportion of the refraction for this section be, as before, that of the line *N* to the **semidiameter** <note>The "semidiameter" refers to what we now call the radius of the circle.</note> *CQ*.

Therefore, as *N* is to *CQ*, so is *VB* to *DS*. Let *ML* be perpendicular to *CL*. And because I suppose the eyes *rr* to be distant from the crystal by a foot or thereabouts, and consequently the angle *RSr* very small, *VB* must be considered as equal to the semidiameter *CQ*, and *DP* as equal to *CL*; therefore, as *N* is to *CQ*, so is *CQ* to *DS*. the axis *ss*, as is the plane of this figure, the perpendicular ray should not suffer any <term>refraction</term> <note>Refraction is the "bending" of light as it passes from one material into another, like air into water.</note>; and that for oblique rays, there were different measures for the irregular refraction, according to the orientation of the plane where the incident ray was located.

Now, these things were indeed found to be so, and after that I could not doubt that a similar success would be met everywhere. From this I concluded that one can shape from this crystal pieces similar to those which are natural to it, which will produce, on all their surfaces, the same regular and irregular refractions as the natural surfaces, and which nonetheless will <term>cleave</term> <note>Cleavage is the tendency of a crystal to break along specific smooth planes due to its internal structure.</note> quite differently, and not parallel to any of the faces.

One can also make pyramids from it, having a square, pentagonal, or hexagonal base, or with as many sides as one wishes, of which all the surfaces have the same refractions as the natural surfaces of the crystal, except for the base, which will not refract the perpendicular ray. These surfaces will each make an angle of 45 deg. 20 min. with the <term>axis</term> <note>The "axis" of the crystal is the direction along which light behaves most simply; in Iceland spar, it connects the blunt corners of the crystal shape.</note> of the crystal, and the base will be the section perpendicular to the axis.

Finally, one can also make triangular prisms from it, or of as many sides as one wants, of which neither the sides nor the bases will refract the perpendicular ray, although they nonetheless all produce <term>double refraction</term> for oblique rays. The cube is included among these prisms; its bases are sections perpendicular to the axis of the crystal, and the sides are sections parallel to this same axis.

From all of this, it further appears that the cause of its irregular refraction does not reside at all in the arrangement of the layers of which this crystal appears to be composed—and along which it cleaves in three different directions—and that it would be in vain to wish to seek it there.

But

<summary>Huygens concludes that his wave theory accurately predicts how light behaves in Iceland spar regardless of how the crystal is cut. He dismisses the idea that light is bent by the physical layers of the stone, arguing instead that it is a result of the light waves' internal structure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Huygens' discussion on the practicalities of working with Iceland spar. Having previously established the mathematical rules for its double refraction, he now transitions from theory to experimental practice, explaining how he prepared the physical specimens used for his observations.</meta>

# T R E A T I S E

But so that everyone who possesses this stone may find, through their own experience, the truth of what I have just put forward, I will describe here the manner in which I have proceeded to cut and polish it. The cutting is easy using the sharp wheels of <term>lapidaries</term> <note>Craftspeople who cut, polish, or engrave gemstones.</note>, or in the same way that one saws marble; but the <term>polish</term> <note>The process of smoothing a surface until it is reflective or transparent.</note> is very difficult, and by employing ordinary means, one ruins the surface much sooner than making it shiny.

After several trials, I finally found that no metal plate is needed for this purpose, but rather a piece of mirror glass that has been made matte and unpolished. Upon this, with fine sand and water, one gradually smooths this <term>crystal</term> <note>Referring to Iceland spar (calcite), which Huygens frequently calls "crystal" throughout the work.</note> just as one does for eyeglass lenses, and one polishes it simply by continuing the work and always decreasing the grit <note>Literally "decreasing the material," meaning using finer and finer abrasive substances.</note>. I have not, however, been able to give it perfect clarity and transparency; but the evenness acquired by the surfaces allows one to observe the effects of <term>refraction</term> <note>The bending of light as it passes from one material (like air) into another (like the crystal).</note> better there than in surfaces made by splitting the stone, which always have some irregularity.

Even when the surface is only moderately smoothed, if one rubs it with a little oil or egg white, it becomes very transparent, so that the refraction reveals itself very distinctly. And this aid is especially necessary when one wants to polish the natural surfaces to remove their irregularities; because one cannot make them as shiny as those of the other sections, which take a polish all the better the further they are from these natural planes.

Before finishing the treatise on this Crystal, I will add yet another marvelous phenomenon, which I discovered after having written all of the above. For although I have not been able to find the cause of it until now, I do not wish for that reason to omit pointing it out, in order to give others the occasion to seek it. It seems that it would be necessary to make still other suppositions besides those

This leads to his discovery of the polarization of light.</meta>

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

...that I have made; which will nonetheless retain all their plausibility, after having been confirmed by so many proofs.

The phenomenon is that, by taking two pieces of this **crystal** <note>Iceland spar, or calcite, known for its double-refraction properties.</note> and placing them one upon the other, or indeed holding them with a space between the two; if all the sides of one are parallel to those of the other, then a **ray of light** <note>Huygens conceptualizes light as waves, but uses "rays" to describe the path of the wave fronts.</note>, such as AB, having been divided in two in the first piece—namely into BD and BC, following the two refractions, **regular** and **irregular**—upon penetrating from there into the other piece, each ray will pass through without further splitting into two. Instead, that which was produced by the **regular refraction** <note>The "ordinary ray" which follows standard optical laws.</note>, as shown here as DG, will only undergo another regular refraction at GH; and the other, CE, an **irregular refraction** <note>The "extraordinary ray" which bends at an unusual angle.</note> at EF. And the same thing happens not only in this arrangement, but also in all those where the **principal section** <note>The geometric plane within the crystal that contains the axis of symmetry.</note>...

These rays continue into a second crystal below, entering at points 'E' and 'G' and exiting at points 'F' and 'H'. The diagrams show how the regular and irregular refractions maintain their character through the second medium when aligned.<-

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

the lower piece, and on the contrary, the ray which came from the irregular refraction, such as CE, now undergoes only a regular refraction.

But in all the other infinite positions, besides those which I have just determined, the rays DG and CE are each divided again into two by the refraction of the lower crystal; so that from the single ray AB, four are produced, sometimes of equal brightness, sometimes some much fainter than others, according to the different alignment of the positions of the crystals: but which do not appear to have more light all together than the single ray AB.

When one considers here that—while the rays CE and DG remain the same—it depends on the position given to the bottom piece whether to divide each of them into two, or not to divide them at all, whereas the ray AB is always divided, it seems that one is obliged to conclude that the waves of light, having passed through the first crystal, acquire a certain form or disposition <note>Huygens is describing what we now call "polarization," though he lacks the specific term for it here.</note>. By this disposition, when encountering the texture of the second crystal in a certain position, they can move the two different matters <note>The "matters" refer to the hypothetical ethers or mediums within the crystal that Huygens believed carried the light waves at different speeds.</note> which serve the two types of refraction; and when encountering this second crystal in another position, they can move only one of these matters. But as for how this is done, I have found nothing until now that satisfies me.

Leaving then to others this research, I pass to what I have to say concerning the cause of the extraordinary figure of this crystal, and why it splits easily in three different directions, parallel to any of its surfaces.

There are several vegetable bodies, minerals, and frozen salts <note>original: "fels congelez," referring to crystallized salts.</note> which form with certain angles and regular figures. Thus among flowers there are many which have their petals arranged in ordered polygons, in the number of 3, 4, 5, or 6 sides; but no more. This well deserves to be re-

Rock crystal <term>Rock crystal: clear, colorless quartz</term> usually grows in hexagonal columns, and one finds diamonds that are born with a square point and polished surfaces. There is a species of small flat stones, piled directly one upon the other, which are all pentagonal in shape, with rounded angles and sides slightly bent inward. The grains of gray salt, which are born from sea water, affect the shape, or at least the angle, of the cube; and in the congealing of other salts, and that of sugar, one finds other solid angles with perfectly flat surfaces. Fine snow almost always falls formed into little stars with 6 points, and sometimes in hexagons whose sides are straight. And I have often observed, within water that is beginning to freeze, a sort of flat and delicate leaf of ice, whose middle streak throws out branches inclined at an angle of 60 degrees. All 

<column-break/>

these things deserve to be researched carefully, to recognize how and by what artifice nature operates there. But it is not now my design to treat this matter entirely. It seems that in general the regularity, which is found in these productions, comes from the arrangement of the small invisible and equal particles of which they are composed. And to come to our Iceland Crystal <note>Iceland Spar, a transparent variety of calcite known for its double refraction</note>, I say that if there were a pyramid like A B C D, composed of small round corpuscles <note>The term "corpuscle" was used in the 17th century to describe atoms or minute particles of matter</note>, not spherical, but flat spheroids <note>Spheroids: shapes resembling a flattened sphere, like a lentil or a disc</note>, such as would be made by the conversion of this

I say, then, that the solid angle <note>A "solid angle" refers to the three-dimensional corner where multiple planes meet, such as the tip of a pyramid.</note> of the point D would be equal to the obtuse and equilateral angle of this Crystal. I say further, that if these corpuscles <note>Huygens uses "corpuscles" to describe the tiny, invisible particles that make up matter.</note> were lightly glued together, then in breaking this pyramid, it would break along faces parallel to those which form its point: and that by this means, as it is easy to see, it would produce prisms similar to those of the same Crystal, such as this other figure represents. The reason is, that in breaking in this fashion, an entire layer easily separates from its neighboring layer, because each spheroid detaches only from the three spheroids of the other layer; of which three there is only one that touches it by the flattened surface, and the other two

<column-break/>

only by the edges. And the reason that the surfaces separate clean and polished is that if any spheroid of the neighboring layer tried to come out of it to attach itself to the one being separated, it would have to detach itself from six other spheroids that hold it tight, and of which four press upon it by these flattened surfaces. Since, therefore, both the angles of our crystal and the manner in which it cleaves <note>original: "se fend" - referring to the natural tendency of crystals to split along definite planes.</note> agree exactly with what is observed in the composition of such spheroids, there is great reason to believe that its particles are formed and arranged in the same way. There is even enough appearance that the prisms of this crystal are made by the breaking of pyramids, since Mr. Bartholin <note>Erasmus Bartholin (1625–1698), the Danish scientist who first discovered the double refraction of light in Iceland Spar.</note>

# ON LIGHT. CHAP. V.

that each of the spheroids <note>The author, Christiaan Huygens, imagines the crystal is built of tiny flattened spheres or ellipsoids to explain its optical properties.</note> of a layer is hardly held at all by the six others of the same layer surrounding it, because they only touch it by their edges; so that it adheres easily to the neighboring layer, and others to it, for the same reason; which causes uneven surfaces. We also see by experience, that by grinding the crystal on a somewhat rough stone, directly upon the equilateral solid angle, one finds in truth much ease in reducing it in this direction, but much difficulty afterwards in polishing the surface that has been flattened in this manner.

For the other division following the plane G H K L, one will see that each spheroid would have to detach itself from four of the neighboring layer, of which two touch it by the flattened surfaces, and two by the edges. So that this division is likewise more difficult than that which is made parallel to one of the surfaces of the crystal; where we have said that each spheroid only detaches itself from three of its neighboring layer; of which there is only one that touches it by the flattened surface, and the two others by the edges only.

However, what made me realize that there are layers of this latter fashion in the crystal, is that in a piece of half a pound that I have, one sees that it is split all along its length, just like the aforementioned prism through the plane G H K L; which appears by the colors of the Iris <note>Iridescent or rainbow-like colors caused by light interference in a thin crack.</note> spread throughout this plane, although the two pieces still hold together. All this proves, then, that the composition of the crystal is such as we have described. To which I further add this experiment: that if one passes a knife by scraping over any of these natural surfaces, and if it be in descending from the equilateral obtuse angle—that is to say, from the point of the pyramid—one finds it very hard; but by scraping in the opposite direction one cuts into it easily. This follows manifestly from the position of the small spheroids, upon which,

<header>TREATISE</header>

which, in the first manner, the knife slips; but in the other, it catches them from underneath, much like the scales of a fish.

I shall not undertake to say anything regarding the manner in which so many small corpuscles <note>The author refers to the tiny particles or "atoms" he imagines make up the crystal.</note> are engendered, all equal and similar, nor how they are placed in such a beautiful order. Whether they are formed first and then assembled, or whether they arrange themselves thus at birth and as they are produced, which seems more likely to me. To develop such hidden truths would require a knowledge of nature much greater than that which we possess. I will only add that these small spheroids <note>Oval-shaped particles.</note> might well contribute to forming the spheroids of the waves of light, previously supposed; both being situated in the same way, and with their axes parallel.

He says he measured these angles directly on the crystal; which is difficult to do with ultimate precision, because the edges, such as CA and CB in this figure, are usually worn and not perfectly straight. For greater certainty, therefore, I preferred to actually measure the angle

I found this angle OCN to be 105 degrees, and its supplement to two right angles <note>The supplement to 180 degrees.</note>, CNP, to be 75 degrees, as was required.

To find the obtuse angle BCA from this, I imagined a sphere having its center at C, and on its surface a spherical triangle formed by the intersection of the three planes that comprise the solid angle C. In this equilateral triangle, which is ABF in this other figure, I saw that each of the angles must be 105 degrees—namely, equal to the angle OCN—and that each of the sides consisted of as many degrees as the angle ACB, ACF, or BCF. Having then drawn the arc FQ perpendicular to the side AB, which it divides equally at Q, the triangle FQA had a right angle at Q, the angle A was 105 degrees, and F was half as much—namely, 52 degrees, 30 minutes. From this, the hypotenuse AF is found to be 101 degrees, 52 minutes. And this arc AF is the measure of the angle ACF in the figure of the crystal.

Now, the arc FQ is found to be 109 degrees, 3 minutes. Therefore its supplement, 70 degrees, 57 minutes, is the angle CFH.

->*Lemma which has been assumed.*<-

If a spheroid is touched by a straight line, and also by two or more planes which are parallel to that line, although not parallel to each other; all the points of contact, both of the line and of the planes, will be in the same ellipse, formed by a plane passing through the center of the spheroid.

Let the spheroid $LED$ be touched by the line $BM$ at point $B$, and also by planes, parallel to this line, at points $O$ and $A$. It must be demonstrated that the points $B$, $O$, and $A$ are in the same Ellipse, formed in the spheroid by a plane passing through its center.

Through the line $BM$ and through the points $O$ and $A$, let there be drawn planes parallel to each other, 

<column-break/>

which, by cutting the spheroid, form the ellipses $LBD$, $POP$, $QAQ$; which will all be similar, and similarly positioned, and will have their centers $K$, $N$, $R$, in the same diameter of the spheroid, which will also be the diameter of the ellipse formed by the section of the plane which passes through the center of the spheroid, and which cuts the planes of the three aforementioned Ellipses at right angles;

for all this is manifest by proposition 15, of the book of <term>Archimedes' Conoids and Spheroids</term> <note>A foundational Greek mathematical work by Archimedes of Syracuse (c. 287–212 BC) concerning the volumes and areas of solids of revolution.</note>. Furthermore, the two latter

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the propagation of light waves within a spheroid medium, specifically focusing on how parallel tangent planes relate to the centers of elliptical sections.</meta>

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER V.

The last planes, which were drawn through the points O and A, will also—by cutting the planes that touched the spheroid at these same points—be straight lines, such as O H and A S. These will be, as is easy to see, parallel to B M; and all three, B M, O H, and A S, will touch the Ellipses L B D, P O P, and Q A Q at these points B, O, and A; since they lie within the planes of these ellipses, and at the same time within the planes that touch the spheroid. Now, if from these points B, O, and A, one draws straight lines B K, O N, and A R through the centers of the same ellipses, and if through these centers one also draws the diameters L D, P P, and Q Q parallel to the tangents B M, O H, and A S: these diameters will be the <term>conjugates</term> <note>In geometry, conjugate diameters of an ellipse are pairs of diameters where each is parallel to the tangent lines at the endpoints of the other.</note> of the aforementioned B K, O N, and A R.

And because the three ellipses are similar, and similarly positioned, and have their diameters L D, P P, and Q Q parallel, it is certain that their conjugate diameters B K, O N, and A R will also be parallel. And the centers K, N, and R being, as has been stated, on the same diameter of the spheroid, these parallels B K, O N, and A R will necessarily be in the same plane, which passes through this diameter of the spheroid: and consequently the points B, O, and A are in the same ellipse formed by the intersection of this plane. Which was to be proved. And it is manifest that the demonstration would be the same if, besides the points O and A, there were others in which the spheroid was touched by planes parallel to the straight line B M.

### CHAPTER VI.
ON THE FIGURES OF TRANSPARENT BODIES
*Which serve for Refraction and Reflection.*

After having explained how the properties of reflection and refraction follow from what we have

# TREATISE

Having established the nature of light, and of opaque and <term>diaphanous</term><note>Transparent or translucent materials, such as glass or water.</note> bodies, I shall show here a very easy and natural way to deduce, from the same principles, the true figures which serve, either by reflection or by refraction, to collect or to disperse the rays of light as one desires. For although I do not see that there is a means of making use of these figures in the matter of Refraction—both because of the difficulty of shaping eyeglass lenses according to them with the required accuracy, and because there is in refraction itself a property which prevents the perfect meeting of rays, as Mr. Newton has very well proved by experiments—I shall not fail to report the invention of them, since it offers itself, so to speak, of its own accord, and since it further confirms our Theory of refraction by the agreement found here between the <term>broken ray</term><note>The refracted ray; "broken" refers to the change in direction as light passes from one medium to another.</note> and the reflected one. Besides, it may be that some uses will be discovered in the future that are not presently seen.

To come then to these figures, let us first suppose that one wishes to find a surface C D E which assembles the rays coming from a point A to another point B: and let the summit of the surface be the point D, given in the straight line A B. I say that, whether by reflection or by refraction, it is only necessary to make this surface such that the path of light, from point A to all points of the curved line C D E, and from these to the point of meeting—as is here the path through the straight lines A C, C B, through A L, L B, and through A D, D B—is made everywhere in equal times: by which the invention of these curves becomes very easy.

For as far as the reflecting surface is concerned, since the sum of the lines A C, C B must be equal to that of A D, D B, it appears that D C E must be an ellipse; and for refraction, having supposed the proportion of the speeds of the waves of light in the transparent media A and B to be known, for example, as 3 to 2 (which is the same...

Since the sums of the times <note>The author is applying Fermat's Principle of Least Time, though here expressed through wave fronts.</note> through A K, K B, and through A C, C B are equal; if from the first sum one takes away the time through K B, and from the other the time through R B; there will remain the time through A K equal to the time through these two, A C, C R. Consequently, in the time that the light has traveled through A K, it will also have traveled through C, and furthermore, a particular spherical wave will have been formed within the <term>transparent medium</term> <note>original: "diaphane"; refers to any substance like glass or water that allows light to pass.</note>, from the center C, and whose radius will be equal to C R; which wave will necessarily touch the circumference K S at K, since C B cuts this circumference at right angles. Likewise, having taken some other point L in the curve, it will be shown that in the same time as the passage of light through A K, it will also have traveled through A L, and that furthermore, a particular wave will have been formed from the center L, which will touch the same circumference K S. And so for all the other points of the curve C D E.

Therefore, at the moment that the light has arrived at K, the arc K R S will terminate the movement which has spread from A onto D C K. And thus this same arc will be, within the transparent medium, the propagation of the wave emanating from point A; which wave one can represent by the arc D N, or by some other closer to the center A. But all parts of the arc K R S are subsequently extended along straight lines which are perpendicular to it, that is to say, which tend toward the center B (for this is demonstrated in the same way as we have proved above that the parts of spherical waves extend along straight lines coming from their center) and these progressions of the parts of the waves are the rays of light themselves. It appears, therefore, that all these rays tend here toward point B.

Only one part of this oval serves for refraction; namely, if A K is supposed to be the tangent, it will be the part D X, of which the limit is K. As for the other part, Descartes remarked that it would serve for refractions if there were some mirror material of such a nature that through it the force of the rays (we shall say the speed of light, which he could not say because he maintains that its movement occurs in an instant <note>Huygens is critiquing Descartes' belief that light travels instantaneously; Huygens' own theory relies on light having a finite, measurable speed.</note>) were increased in the proportion of 3 to 2. But we have shown that, in our manner of explaining reflection, this cannot come from the material of the mirror, and that it is entirely impossible.

From what has been demonstrated regarding this oval, it will be easy to find the figure that serves to collect parallel incident rays toward a point. For by supposing the entire same construction, but with point A infinitely distant—which produces parallel rays—our oval becomes a true Ellipse; the construction of which differs in no way from that of the oval, except that F C is here a straight line, perpendicular to D B, which previously was an arc of a circle. For the wave of light D N, being likewise represented by a straight line, it will be shown that all points of this wave, extending to the surface K D by lines parallel to D B, will then advance toward point B, and arrive there at the same time. # ON LIGHT. CHAPTER VI.

Let us now return to our method, and see how it leads without difficulty to finding the lines required for one side of the glass <note>The author uses "glass" to refer to a lens.</note>, when the other is of a given shape; not only flat or spherical, or made by one of the Conic sections (which is the restriction with which Descartes proposed this problem, leaving the solution to those who would come after him) but generally of any shape: that is to say, one made by the revolution of any given curved line, to which one only knows how to draw straight tangent lines.

Let the given figure be made by the rotation of some such curve AK around the axis AV, and let this side of the glass receive rays coming from point L. Let, furthermore, the thickness AB of the middle of the glass be given, and the point F at which one wants the rays to be all perfectly reunited; whatever the first refraction may have been, made at the surface AK.

I say that for this, it is only necessary that the line BDK, which forms the other surface, be such that the path of light, from point L to the surface AK, and from there to the surface BDK, and from there to point F, is made everywhere in equal times, and each equal to the time that light employs to pass through the straight line LF, of which the part AB is within the glass.

Let LG be a ray falling on the arc AK. Its refraction GV will be given by means of the tangent that one will draw at point G. Now it is necessary to find the point D on GV, such that FD with ⅓ of DG and the straight line GL, are equal to FB with ⅓ of BA and the straight line AL; which, as it appears, make a given length. # ON LIGHT. CHAPTER VI.

is the refraction of the ray L G. Therefore, in the time that this part of the wave has traveled from G to D, the other part which was at S has arrived at E, since G D and S E are equal. But while this latter part advances from E to B, the part of the wave that was at D will have spread its particular wave <note>Huygens is describing his principle where every point on a wave front acts as a source for new spherical "wavelets."</note> into the air, of which the semi-diameter <term>semi-diameter: the radius of a circle or sphere</term> D C (supposing that this wave intersects the straight line D F at C) will be 3/2 of F B, since the speed of light outside the transparent medium <term>diaphane: a transparent substance or medium</term> is to that within as 3 to 2. Now, it is easy to show that this wave will touch the arc B P at this point C. For since, by the construction, F D + 3/2 D G + G L are equal to F B + 3/2 B A + A L; by subtracting the equals L H and L A, there will remain F D + 3/2 D G + G H, equal to F B + 3/2 B A. And again, subtracting from one side G H, and from the other side A S, which are equal, there will remain F D with 3/2 of D G, equal to F B with 3/2 of B S. But 3/2 of D G is equal to 3/2 of E S; therefore F D is equal to F B with 3/2 of B E. But D C was equal to 3/2 of F B; therefore subtracting these equal lengths from both sides, there will remain C F equal to F B; and thus it appears that the wave, whose semi-diameter is D C, touches the arc B P at the moment that the light, coming from point L, has arrived at B by the straight line L B. One shall demonstrate in the same way that at this same moment, the light coming through every other ray, such as L M, M N, will have spread movement which is terminated by the arc B P. From which it follows, as has been often said, that the propagation of the wave A H, after having passed through the thickness of the glass, will be the spherical wave B P: from which all parts must advance along straight lines, which are the rays of light, toward the center F. Which was to be demonstrated.

One will find these curved lines in the same way in all cases that can be proposed, as will be seen clearly enough by one or two examples that I shall add.

It is therefore necessary that the wave <term>T G</term><note>Huygens uses letters to denote specific points and segments in his geometric diagrams of light waves.</note>, after having passed through the glass, forms the wave <term>Q D</term>; and for this I see that the time taken by the light through <term>G D</term> inside the glass must be equal to that taken through these three segments <term>T A</term>, <term>A B</term>, and <term>B Q</term>, of which only <term>A B</term> is also within the glass. Which last difference is a given length: and it is only necessary, from the given point <term>F</term>, to draw the straight line <term>F D</term> onto <term>V G</term>, in such a way that this is found to be so. Which is a problem entirely similar to that which serves the first of these constructions, where <term>F D</term> + ½ <term>G D</term> had to be equal to a given length.

# ON LIGHT. CHAPTER VI.

A notable point concerning the irregular refraction <note>The term "refraction" refers to the bending of light as it passes from one medium, like air, into another, like glass.</note> of spherical, plane, and other surfaces; which, if ignored, could cause some doubt regarding what we have said several times: that light rays are straight lines which intersect the waves spreading from them at right angles. For example, rays falling parallel onto a spherical surface AFE intersect one another at different points after their refraction, as represented in this figure; what then could

<column-break/>

be the waves of light in this transparent medium <note>original: "diaphane"</note> that are intersected at right angles by the converging rays? For they could not be spherical; and what will become of these waves after the said rays begin to intersect one another? One will see, in the solution to this difficulty, that something very remarkable occurs here, and that the waves do not cease to exist always; although they do not pass through whole, as they do through *compound* <note>The author refers to "verres compofez," likely meaning lens systems designed to focus light perfectly to a single point.</note> glasses, the construction of which we have just seen.

## TREATISE

According to what has been shown above, the straight line AD—which is drawn from the summit of the sphere perpendicular to its axis, and towards which the rays arrive parallel—represents the wave of light. In the time that its point D has reached the spherical surface AGE at E, its other parts will have met the same surface at F, G, H, etc., and will have further formed particular spherical waves <note>Huygens is describing "secondary wavelets," a core part of his principle that every point on a wavefront acts as a source for new waves.</note> of which these points are the centers. And the surface EK, which all these waves touch, will be the propagation of the wave AD within the sphere at the moment that point D has arrived at E. Now, the line EK is not an arc of a circle, but is a curved line made by the *Evolution* <term>Evolution: the process of unwinding a curve (the evolute) to create another curve (the involute)</term> of another curve BNC, which touches all the rays HL, GM, FO, etc., which are the refractions of the parallel rays. This is imagined by supposing there is a thread lying upon the convexity BNC, which, by unfolding, describes the said curve BK with its end E. For, assuming that this curve is described in this way, we shall demonstrate that the said waves formed from the centers F, G, H, etc., will all touch it.

It is certain that the curve BK, and all others described by the evolution of the curve BNC with different lengths of thread, will cut all the rays HL, GM, FO, etc., at right angles, and in such a way that their parts intercepted between two such curves will all be equal; for this follows from what has been demonstrated in our treatise *On the Motion of Pendulums* <note>original: "Motu Pendulorum." This refers to Huygens' 1673 work *Horologium Oscillatorium*, where he established the theory of evolutes.</note>. Now, imagining the incident rays as infinitely close to one another, if one considers two of them, such as RG and TF, and draws GQ perpendicular to RG, and if the curve FS (which cuts GM at P) is described by the evolution of the curve NC starting from F to where I suppose the thread extends; one can take its particle FP for a straight line perpendicular to the ray GM, and likewise the arc GF as a straight line. To now explain what becomes of these waves after the rays begin to cross each other: it is from that point that they fold back, and are composed of two parts that hold together; one being a curve created by the **evolution** <note>In geometry, an "evolution" or "evolute" refers to the curve formed by the centers of curvature of another curve; here it describes how a wavefront unfolds.</note> of the curve E N C in one direction, and the other by the evolution of the same in the other direction. Thus the wave K E, in advancing toward the meeting point, becomes *a b c*, of which the part *a b* is made by the evolution of *b c*, a portion of the curve E N C, while the end *c* remains attached; and the part *b c* by the evolution of the portion B E, while the end E remains attached. Subsequently, the same wave becomes *d e f*; then *g h k*; and finally *c y*; from where it then extends without any fold, but always along curved lines, which are formed by the evolution of the curve E N C, augmented by some straight line on the side of *c*.

There is even, in this curve here, a part E N which is straight, N being the point where the perpendicular falls from the center of the sphere X, onto the refraction of the ray D E, which I now suppose touches the sphere. And it is from the point N that the folding of the light waves begins, up to the extremity of the curve *c*; which is found by making A C to C X be in the proportion of the refraction, as here from 3 to 2 <note>This 3:2 ratio is the common approximation for the refractive index of glass relative to air.</note>.

One also finds as many other points as one wishes of the curve N C by a **Theorem** <term>Theorem: a mathematical statement that has been proven based on previously established statements</term> demonstrated by **Mr. Barrow** <note>Isaac Barrow (1630–1677), an English theologian and mathematician who was Isaac Newton's teacher.</note> in the 12th of his *Optical Lectures*, though for another purpose. And it is to be noted that one can provide a straight line equal to this curve. For since together with the straight line N E, it is equal to the straight line C K, which is known, because D E to A K is in the proportion of the refraction: it appears that by taking E N away from C K, the remainder will be equal to the curve N C.

->Of a concave spherical mirror.<- Let ABC be the section through the axis of a hollow hemisphere, whose center is D, and the axis is DB, to which I suppose the rays of light come parallel. All the reflections of these rays, which fall upon the quadrant AB, will touch a curved line AFE, whose end E is at the focus <note>The "foyer" or focus is the point where light rays converge after reflection.</note> of the hemisphere—that is to say, at the point which divides the semidiameter <term>semidiameter: the radius of the circle</term> BD into two equal parts: and the points through which this curve must pass are found by taking some arc AO, and making the arc OP double to it; of which one must divide the chord <note>original: "foutendente" (subtendente), referring to the straight line segment connecting two points on a curve.</note> at F, in such a way that the part FP is triple that of FO; for then F is one of the required points.

## PREFACE

Nature acts by ways so secret and so imperceptible, in bringing toward the Earth those bodies which we call heavy <note>original: "pesans"; referring to the quality of weight or gravity</note>, that whatever attention or industry one employs, the senses are unable to discover anything there. This is what has obliged the Philosophers of past centuries to seek the cause of this admirable effect only within the bodies themselves, and to attribute it to some internal and inherent quality which made them tend downward and toward the center of the Earth, or to an appetite of the parts to unite with the whole. This was not to explain the causes, but to suppose obscure and misunderstood Principles. One can forgive those who contented themselves with such solutions in many instances; but not so easily Democritus <note>Greek philosopher (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) known for the atomic theory of the universe</note> and those of his Sect, who having undertaken to give a reason for everything by Atoms, have excepted only Gravity; which they have attached to the

<sig>R 2</sig>
<vocab>Nature, Earth, Philosophers, Principles, Democritus, Sect, Atoms, Gravity</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces the mystery of gravity, critiquing ancient philosophers and Democritus for attributing the downward fall of objects to inherent "appetites" or unexplained internal qualities rather than mechanical causes.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, physics, Democritus, atomism, natural philosophy, history of science, heavy bodies</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---

# PREFACE.

to terrestrial bodies, and even to atoms, without inquiring from whence it could come to them. Among the modern authors and restorers of Philosophy, several have rightly judged that it was necessary to establish something outside of bodies to cause the attractions and sequences observed in them: but they go hardly any further than those first thinkers when they have recourse, some to a subtle and heavy air, which by pressing upon bodies makes them descend; (for this is already to suppose a gravity <note>The author argues that using "heavy air" to explain gravity is circular reasoning, as it assumes the existence of weight to explain weight.</note>, and it is so strongly against the laws of Mechanics <term>Mechanics: the branch of physics dealing with the motion of objects and the forces acting on them</term> to claim that a liquid and heavy matter presses down the bodies it surrounds, when on the contrary it ought to make them rise, being supposed without any weight in themselves, just as water makes an empty vial rise when one plunges it in:) others to spirits and immaterial emanations; which clarifies nothing, since we have no conception of how that which is immaterial gives motion to a corporeal substance.

Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher and mathematician who sought to explain the universe through mechanical interactions.</note> has better recognized than those who preceded him, that one would never understand anything more in Physics than what could be related to Principles that do not exceed the reach of our mind, such as those which depend on bodies, considered without qualities <note>original: "qualitez" — referring to "occult qualities" or hidden properties that cannot be measured.</note>, and on their movements. But as the greatest difficulty

# P R E F A C E.

## P R E F A C E.

The difficulty consists in showing how so many diverse things are brought about by these <term>Principles</term> <note>Fundamental laws of nature used to explain physical phenomena</note> alone; it is in this that he has not succeeded very well in several particular subjects he proposed to examine: among which is, in my opinion, that of <term>Gravity</term> <note>original: "Pesanteur." In this period, gravity was often explained through the motion of fluids or ethers rather than universal attraction.</note>. One may judge this by the remarks I make in several places regarding what he has written on it, to which I could have added others. And yet, I confess that his attempts and his views, although false, have served to open the path for me toward what I have discovered on this same subject.

I do not present this as being exempt from all doubt, nor as something to which one cannot raise objections. It is too difficult to reach that point in research of this nature. I believe, however, that if the principal hypothesis upon which I base myself is not the true one, there is little hope that it can be found while remaining within the limits of true and sound <term>Philosophy</term> <note>Natural philosophy, the precursor to modern physics.</note>.

Furthermore, what I contribute here, insofar as it only concerns the cause of <term>Gravity</term>, will not appear new to those who have read the Treatise on <term>Physics</term> by Mr. <term>Rohault</term> <note>Jacques Rohault (1618–1672), a prominent Cartesian physicist whose textbook was widely used.</note>; because my <term>Theory</term> is reported there almost in its entirety. For this Philosopher, having seen my <term>Experiment of the rotating water</term> <note>A demonstration using centrifugal force to explain how heavier bodies are pushed toward a center by the displacement of lighter, faster-moving matter.</note>, and having heard the application I made of it (just as he

->R 3<-

recognizes with ingenuity <note>original: "ingenuité"; here meaning frankness or candor</note>,) has found enough probability <note>original: "vraisemblance"</note> in my opinion to wish to follow it. But because among my thoughts, he somewhat mixes those of Mr. Descartes and his own, and because he omits several things belonging to this matter—some of which he could not have known—I was very glad that people should see how I have treated it myself.

The greatest part of this Discourse was written during the time I lived in Paris, and it is in the Registers of the Royal Academy of Sciences <note>The French Academy of Sciences, founded in 1666, of which Huygens was a founding member</note>, up to the point where the alteration of Pendulums by the movement of the Earth is discussed. The rest was added several years later: and subsequently the Addition was also included, for the reason that will be found indicated at its beginning.

# DISCOURSE
### On the Cause
## OF GRAVITY.

<insert>I</insert>n order to find an intelligible cause for Gravity <note>original: "Pesanteur"; in this context, it refers to the force that draws objects toward the center of the Earth</note>, we must see how it is possible—while assuming that in nature there are only bodies made of the same matter, in which we consider no quality nor any inclination to approach one another, but only different sizes, shapes, and movements—how, I say, it is possible that many of these bodies nevertheless tend directly toward the same center, and remain gathered around it, which is the most common and principal phenomenon of what we call gravity.
The simplicity of the principles I admit does not leave much choice in this investigation. For one judges immediately that there is no likelihood of attributing to the shape, nor to the smallness of corpuscles <note>term: corpuscles (minute particles of matter that make up larger bodies)</note>, any effect similar to that of gravity; which, being an exertion or an inclination toward movement, must likely be produced by a movement. So that it only remains to seek in what manner it can act, and in which bodies it may be found.

<sig>S</sig>
<sig>3. A</sig>

<vocab>Gravity, Cause, bodies, matter, sizes, shapes, movements, center, phenomenon, principles, corpuscles, exertion, inclination</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a mechanical explanation for gravity, arguing that it must be caused by the motion of matter rather than inherent qualities or the shapes of particles. The author seeks to explain why bodies are drawn toward a common center using only the principles of size, shape, and motion.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, mechanical philosophy, corpuscular theory, laws of motion, physics, center of attraction</keywords>

-> DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE <-

### DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE

If we look simply at bodies, without that quality we call gravity <note>original: "pesanteur"; here referring to the weight or the downward pull of objects</note>, their motion is naturally either straight or circular. The first belongs to them when they move without hindrance; the other when they are held around some center, or when they turn upon their own center. We understand to some extent the nature of straight motion, and the laws that bodies observe in the communication of their movements when they encounter one another. But as long as we consider only this sort of motion, and the reflections that occur between the parts of matter, we find nothing that determines them to tend toward a center. It is therefore necessary to turn to the properties of circular motion, and see if there is any among them that can serve us.

<margin>•</margin> I know that Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher and mathematician who proposed a "vortex" theory of gravity</note> also endeavored in his Physics to explain gravity by the motion of certain matter that turns around the Earth; and it is a great deal to have been the first to have had this thought. But one will see, by the remarks I shall make in the course of this discourse, in what way his method is different from the one I am going to propose, and also in what way it seemed to me defective.

He considered, as I have, the effort made by bodies that turn circularly to move away from the center; a fact which experience does not allow us to doubt. For in turning a stone in a sling, one feels that it pulls the hand, and this all the more strongly as one turns more quickly; even to the point that the cord may come to break. I have previously shown this same property of circular motion by attaching heavy bodies to a round table, pierced at the center, and which turned on a pivot; and I found the determination of its force, and several theorems <term>Theoremes: mathematical propositions that have been proved</term> concerning it: which can be seen at the end of the book I wrote on the *Motion of Pendulums* <note>original: "Mouvement des Pendules"; referring to Huygens' 1673 work "Horologium Oscillatorium"</note>. For example, I say that a body turning in a circle at the end of a cord

<summary>The author explores the mechanical origins of gravity, arguing that it must result from the properties of circular motion rather than straight motion. He acknowledges Descartes' earlier vortex theory while preparing to present his own improved mathematical model based on centrifugal force.</summary>

horizontal cord, if it moves with the speed it could acquire through its fall, by falling from a height equal to half of that same cord—that is to say, to a quarter of the diameter of the circumference it describes—the cord will be pulled with exactly as much force as if it were supporting the same body suspended in the air. <note>This describes the formula for centrifugal force, relating the tension in a string to the velocity and radius of a circular path.</note>

The effort to move away from the center is therefore a constant effect of circular motion. And although this effect seems directly opposed to that of gravity, and although it was objected to Copernicus <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proposed the heliocentric model where the Earth rotates on its axis.</note> that, by the rotation of the earth in 24 hours, houses and men ought to be thrown into the air; I shall nonetheless show that this same effort, made by bodies turning in a circle to move away from the center, is the cause of other bodies converging toward that same center.

I also remarked that this powder proceeded toward the center along spiral lines, because the water still dragged it along slightly. But if one adjusts some body within this vessel in such a way that it cannot follow the motion of the water at all, but can only move toward the center, it will then be pushed there in a straight line. For example, if **L** is a small ball that can roll freely on the bottom between the threads **AA**, **BB**, and a third slightly higher one **KK**, stretched horizontally through the middle of the vessel; one will see that as soon as the motion of the vessel is stopped, this ball will go to the center **D**. And it must be noted that, in this last experiment, one can make the body **L** of the same weight <note>original: "pesanteur"</note> as the water, and the thing will succeed even better; so that, without any difference in the weight of the bodies within the vessel, motion alone produces the effect here.

The experiment that Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), whose vortex theory of gravity the author is critiquing.</note> proposes, in one of his printed letters, differs greatly from this one. For he fills the vessel **ABC** with small lead shot, intermixed with some pieces of wood or other material lighter than lead: and making it all turn together, he says that the pieces of wood will be driven toward the middle of the vase. This I can well believe, provided however that one strikes lightly on the edges of the vessel to facilitate the separation of these two materials. But what happens here is in no way fit to represent the effect of gravity; since one would have to conclude from this experiment that the bodies containing the least matter are those that weigh the most—which is contrary to what is observed in true gravity. He proposes yet another, in another

letter, to throw small pieces of wood into swirling water, and he says that they will move toward the middle of the water. At which point, if he means wood that floats on the water, as it appears, no concentration <note>The gathering of matter toward a central point</note> will occur. But if he intends for it to go to the bottom, it will truly be the same experiment that I proposed shortly before, and the wood will gather at the center; however, this will be because its circular motion will be slowed by touching the bottom of the vessel, a reason of which Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), whose mechanical theory of "vortices" attempted to explain gravity without "action at a distance."</note> did not speak at all.

Now, having found in nature an effect similar to that of gravity <note>original: "pesanteur," referring here to the weight or the downward pull of objects</note>, and of which the cause is known, it remains to be seen if one can suppose that something similar happens in regard to the Earth; that is to say, whether there is some movement of matter that compels bodies to tend toward the center, and which accommodates itself at the same time to all the other phenomena of gravity.

Supposing the daily rotation of the Earth, and that the air and the ether <note>The "aether," a hypothetical fluid substance then thought to fill all space</note> surrounding it have this same motion, there is still nothing in that which ought to produce gravity: since, following the experiment reported shortly before, terrestrial bodies should not follow this circular motion of the celestial matter, but be as if at rest in relation to it, if it were necessary for them to be pushed by it toward the center.

But if one wished for the celestial matter to turn in the same direction as the Earth, but with much more speed, it would follow that this rapid movement—of a matter moving continually and all in the same direction—would make itself felt, and that it would carry away with it the bodies that are on the Earth; just as the water carries away the sealing wax <note>original: "cire d'Espagne," a hard wax used for sealing letters, used here as a test object in a fluid dynamics experiment</note> in our experiment; which, however, does not happen at all. But besides that, this circular motion around the axis of the Earth could not, in any case, drive the bodies that do not follow the same...

<summary>The author critiques Descartes's theory of gravity, arguing that circular motion in a fluid only drives objects to the center if their motion is slowed by friction, and questions how celestial matter could cause gravity without dragging objects along with it.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, René Descartes, circular motion, celestial matter, ether, fluid dynamics, physics experiments</keywords>
