<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of gravity, specifically refuting the idea that gravity is directed toward the Earth's axis rather than its center.</meta>

# ON GRAVITY:

the same movement, except toward this same axis, so that we would not see heavy bodies falling perpendicularly to the horizon, but along lines perpendicular to the axis of the world, which is again contrary to experience <note>The author is arguing against a theory where gravity is caused by the Earth's rotation alone, which would pull objects toward the axis of rotation rather than the center of the sphere.</note>.

To explain gravity in the manner that I conceive it, I will suppose that within the spherical space, which encompasses the Earth and the bodies around it to a great extent, there is a fluid matter consisting of very small parts, which is variously agitated in all directions with great rapidity. Since this matter cannot leave this space, being surrounded by other bodies, I say that its movement must become partly circular around the center; not so much, however, that it all turns in the same direction, but in such a way that most of its different movements occur within spherical surfaces around the center of the said space, which for this reason also becomes the center of the Earth.

The reason for this circular movement is that matter contained within any space moves more easily in this manner than by straight movements contrary to one another, which, even when reflecting back (because the matter cannot leave the space enclosing it), are reduced to changing into circular ones.

One sees this effect of movement when testing silver by the Cupel <note>original: "Coupelle"; a small, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead.</note>; for the small ball of lead mixed with silver, having its parts strongly agitated by the heat, turns incessantly around its center, now to one side, now to another, changing at every moment, and so fast that the eye has difficulty perceiving it. The same thing also happens to a drop of candle tallow when, holding it suspended from the tip of the snuffers <note>original: "mouchettes"; a scissor-like tool used to trim candle wicks.</note>, one brings it near the flame; for it begins to turn with a very great speed.

It is true that usually this drop turns entirely in one

<summary>The author proposes that gravity is caused by the rapid, circular agitation of a fluid matter surrounding the Earth, using the behavior of molten metals and candle tallow as physical analogies.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, fluid matter, circular motion, physics of rotation, cupellation, silver assaying, tallow, experimental observation</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's mechanical explanation of gravity and the behavior of celestial matter, following the Cartesian tradition of physics.</meta>

# DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE

one side or the other, depending on how the flame of the candle happens to touch it. But within the celestial matter <note>The "subtle matter" or ether that early modern scientists believed filled all space.</note> that I have proposed, the same thing should not occur; because once it possesses movement in all directions, that movement must always remain, even if it is changed into a spherical motion. There is no reason why the movement of one part of the matter should prevail over that of the others to make the entire mass turn in a single direction. On the contrary, the law of nature—which I have reported elsewhere—is such that when bodies with diverse agitations meet, the same quantity of movement toward the same side is always preserved.

And although these circular movements, occurring in so many different directions within the same space, seem as though they ought to conflict and frequently hinder one another, the great mobility of the matter—aided by the smallness of its parts, which far surpasses the imagination—allows it to undergo all these different agitations quite easily. One sees, when water has been stirred in a glass vial, how many different movements its parts are capable of; and one must imagine the liquidity of the celestial matter to be incomparably greater than that which we observe in water. Water, being composed of heavy parts heaped one upon the other, becomes sluggish in its movement; whereas celestial matter, moving freely in all directions, very easily receives different impressions through the various encounters of its parts or by the slightest impulse from other bodies. If it were not so, the air would not yield as easily as it does to the movement of our hands. Consequently, one must consider that the circular movements of this fluid matter around the Earth are very often interrupted and changed into others, but that there always remains more of these [circular movements] than those following other paths; this suffices for the present design.

<sig>II</sig>

<summary>The author argues that celestial matter maintains constant circular motion in all directions due to its extreme fluidity and the laws of conservation of motion. He uses the analogy of stirred water in a vial to illustrate how microscopic particles can move complexly without total interference.</summary>

### ON GRAVITY.

It is not difficult now to explain how gravity is produced by this motion. For if, among the fluid matter <note>The "ether" or subtle matter theorized by 17th-century physicists to fill all space.</note> that turns in the space we have supposed, there are found parts much larger than those composing it, or bodies made of a mass of small parts hooked together, and if these bodies do not follow the rapid motion of the said matter, they will necessarily be pushed toward the center of motion. There, they will form the Terrestrial globe if there are enough of them for that purpose, assuming the Earth did not yet exist. And the reason is the same as that which, in the experiment reported above, causes the sealing wax <note>original: "cire d'Espagne"</note> to gather at the center of the vessel. It is therefore in this that gravity likely consists: which one can say is the effort made by the fluid matter—which turns circularly around the center of the Earth in all directions—to move away from that center, and to push into its place the bodies that do not follow this motion.

Now, the reason why heavy bodies, which we see descend through the air, do not follow the spherical motion of the fluid matter is quite manifest; because there being this motion toward all sides, the impulses that a body receives from it succeed one another so suddenly that there is less time between them than would be needed for the body to acquire a perceptible motion. But as this reason alone is not sufficient to prevent the smallest bodies the eye can perceive—such as the specks of dust that flutter in the air—from being driven here and there by the rapidity of this motion, it must be understood that these small bodies do not swim solely in the liquid matter that causes gravity. Rather, besides this matter, there are other substances composed of coarser particles which fill the greater part of the space around us, and even that of the heavens;

<summary>The author explains gravity as a mechanical effect where rapidly rotating fluid matter pushes denser, slower-moving bodies toward the center of the Earth. This theory is supported by an analogy to sealing wax in a rotating vessel and accounts for why even small particles like dust are affected by these forces.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, fluid matter, centrifugal force, terrestrial globe, sealing wax experiment, atmospheric particles, mechanical philosophy, physics</keywords>

heavens; these particles, although variously agitated and reflected among themselves, do not follow the sudden movement of the liquid matter <note>The author refers to the "subtle matter" or "aether" hypothesized by Cartesian physics to explain gravity through mechanical pressure.</note>; because being contiguous, or at a short distance from one another, too great a quantity would have to move at once. We know that around the Earth there are first the particles of air, which we will show shortly to be coarser than those of the fluid matter we have supposed. I say further that there is a matter whose particles are smaller than those of air, but coarser than those of this fluid matter: which is proven by our experiment made with the Machine that empties the air <note>The air pump, a revolutionary scientific instrument in the 17th century used to create a vacuum.</note>. Where one notices the effect of an invisible matter that has weight where there is no air; since it supports water suspended there in a glass tube, the open end of which is immersed in other water: and that it makes water flow there from a curved siphon, just as in the air: provided that the water, in these experiments, has been purged of air; which is done by leaving it for several hours in the vacuum. It appears from this first, that the particles of this heavy and invisible body are smaller than those of air, since they pass through the glass which excludes the air, and that they make their weight perceived there. It appears further that they must be coarser than the particles of the fluid matter which causes gravity <term>gravity: the author uses "pesanteur," referring to the quality of having weight or being pushed toward the center of the Earth</term>, so that the body they compose does not follow the movement of this matter, because by following it, it would not be heavy. There may be around us still other kinds of matters of different degrees of thinness, although all coarser than is the matter which causes gravity. All of which will therefore contribute to preventing the small specks of dust from being carried away by the rapid movement of this matter, because they do not follow this movement themselves. Furthermore, one must not find these different degrees of

# ON GRAVITY.

Of small corpuscles <note>The term "corpuscle" was commonly used in the 17th century to describe minute particles of matter before the modern concept of the atom was fully defined.</note>, nor their extreme smallness. For although we have some inclination to believe that bodies, which are barely visible, are already almost as small as they can possibly be, reason tells us that the same proportion that exists between a mountain and a grain of sand, that grain may have to another small body, and this one again to another, and so on as many times as one wishes.

The extreme smallness of the parts of our fluid matter is also an absolute necessity to account for a significant effect of gravity; which is that heavy bodies, enclosed on all sides in a vessel of glass, metal, or any other material whatsoever, are always found to weigh the same. Thus, it is necessary that the matter which we have said is the cause of gravity passes very freely through all the bodies considered the most solid, and with the same ease as through the air.

This is further confirmed by the fact that, if there were not this freedom of passage, a glass bottle would weigh as much as a massive body of glass of the same size; and all solid bodies of equal volume would weigh equally; since according to our Theory, the gravity of each body is regulated by the quantity of fluid matter that must rise in its place.

This matter therefore passes easily into the interstices <note>The small spaces or gaps between the constituent parts of a solid.</note> of the particles of which bodies are composed, but not through the particles themselves; and what causes the different weights, for example, of stones, metals, etc., is that those among these bodies which are heavier contain more such particles, not in number but in volume: for it is in their place alone that the fluid matter can rise. But because one might doubt whether these particles, being impenetrable to the said matter, are therefore entirely solid: (for if they were not, or even...

<summary>The author argues that the fluid matter causing gravity must consist of extremely small particles capable of passing through solid vessels, explaining why weight remains constant regardless of containment. He posits that a body's weight is determined by the volume of solid particles it contains, which displace this gravitational fluid.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, fluid matter, corpuscles, porosity of matter, displacement, physics of weight, 17th-century science</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the author's argument regarding the nature of gravity and the density of matter, specifically addressing the relationship between a body's mass and its weight. The catchword "ravant" from the previous page connects to the concluding thought on the preceding leaf.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>

being empty, they ought to produce the same effect, for the reason I have just stated) I will demonstrate that they possess this perfect solidity; and that consequently the weight of bodies follows precisely the proportion of the matter that composes them.

I will point out for this purpose what happens in the collision <note>original: "choc"</note> of two bodies, when they meet with a horizontal motion. It is certain that the resistance that bodies offer to being moved horizontally—such as a ball of marble or lead placed on a very smooth table—is not caused by their weight toward the Earth, since lateral movement does not tend to move them away from the Earth, and thus it is in no way contrary to the action of gravity <note>original: "pefanteur," referring here to the downward force or heaviness</note>, which pushes them downward.

There is therefore nothing but the quantity of matter joined together, which each body contains, that produces this resistance: so that if two bodies contain as much as one another, they will *reflect equally* <note>meaning they will bounce back with the same force</note>, or both remain without motion, depending on whether they are hard or soft. But experience shows that whenever two bodies reflect thus equally or stop one another, having come to meet with equal speeds, these bodies are of equal weight: therefore it follows that those which are composed of an equal quantity of matter are also of equal weight. Which was to be demonstrated. 

*

Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the famous French philosopher and mathematician whose mechanical theories of the universe the author is here contesting.</note> was of another opinion in this matter, as also regarding the free passage of the matter which causes gravity through the bodies upon which it acts. For as to this last point, he maintains that this matter is prevented, by the encounter with the Earth, from continuing its movements in a straight line, and that for this reason it moves away from it as much as it can. In which he seems not to have thought of that property of gravity which I pointed out shortly before.

<summary>The author argues that a body's resistance to horizontal motion is determined solely by its quantity of matter (mass), proving that weight is directly proportional to matter. He concludes by critiquing Descartes' theory regarding the movement of gravitational matter through the Earth.</summary>

From which it would follow that lead enclosed in a vial would lose its weight with respect to the vial itself, or at least that this weight would be diminished. Furthermore, by carrying a heavy body to the bottom of a well, or into some deep quarry or mine, it ought to lose much of its weight there. But it has not been found, as far as I know, by experience <note>The author refers to empirical testing or scientific observation.</note> that it loses any of it whatsoever.

As for the other point, Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), whose mechanical philosophy sought to explain gravity through the motion of subtle matter.</note> claims that, although a mass of gold is twenty times heavier than a portion of water of the same size, the gold nevertheless may contain only 4 or 5 times as much matter as the water: firstly because one must deduct (he should rather have said add) an equal weight to both, on account of the air in which they are weighed; and then because water and other liquids have some lightness <note>The term "legereté" here refers to buoyancy or a tendency to rise.</note> with respect to hard bodies, insofar as the parts of the former are in continual motion.

But one can respond to the first of these two reasons, that the weight of the air around us, being to that of water only about as 1 to 800, it will not be a considerable weight that must be added equally to that of the water and the gold, as found by the scale. And as for the other reason, if it were good, it would be necessary that a same portion of water, after being frozen, should weigh much more than when liquid; and likewise metals in a solid mass, more than when they are melted; which is contrary to experience. Besides which, I do not see how he conceived that the movement of the parts of liquid bodies would give them lightness, that is to say, the effort to move away from the center, since for that it would be necessary that this movement be circular around the center of the Earth, or that it be stronger toward the top than toward the bottom, which he never said, but rather

For my part, I have carefully researched the degree of this speed, and I believe I can determine approximately how high it must reach. But I find by my Theory of Circular Motion <note>Huygens refers here to his work on centrifugal force, *De Vi Centrifuga*</note>, which agrees perfectly with experience, that for a body turning in a circle—if one wants its effort to move away from the center to exactly equal the effort of its simple gravity—it must complete each revolution in the same amount of time that a Pendulum, of the length of the semi-diameter of that circle, takes to make two swings. It is therefore necessary to see in how much time a pendulum, the length of the semi-diameter of the Earth, would make these two swings. This is easy by the known property of pendulums, and by the length of the one that beats Seconds, which is 3 feet 8 lines, Paris measure <note>A "line" is an old unit of length, 1/12th of an inch; 3 feet 8 lines is approximately 99.4 cm</note>. And I find that it would require 1 hour 24 minutes for these two vibrations; assuming, according to the exact dimension of Mr. Picard <note>Jean Picard (1620–1682), the French astronomer who first accurately measured the size of the Earth</note>, the semi-diameter of the Earth to be 19,615,800 feet of the same measure. The speed, therefore, of the fluid matter at the surface of the Earth must be equal to that of a body that would go around the Earth in this time of 1 hour, 24 minutes. Which speed is, very nearly, 17 times greater than that of a point under the Equator; which makes the same revolution, with respect to the fixed Stars <note>original: "Estoiles fixes"</note>—as it must be taken here—in 23 hours, 56 minutes. This is apparent by the proportion between this time and that of one hour 24 minutes, which is very nearly as 17 to 1.

I know that this rapidity will seem strange to anyone who wishes to compare it with the movements seen here among us. But that should present no difficulty; and even, in relation to its sphere, or to the size of the Earth, it will not appear extraordinary. For if, for example, looking at a Terrestrial Globe, of those made for the use of Geography,

<note>The author is likely Christiaan Huygens, discussing his "mechanical" theory of gravity, which sought to explain physical forces through the motion and impact of tiny particles rather than "occult" attractions.</note>

There are, moreover, several natural effects that seem to require a matter that is extremely agitated, and which easily penetrates through the pores of bodies. Such is the force of gunpowder, which, upon being ignited, does not take its violent movement from itself, nor from the person who brings the match near it; and consequently, it must come from some other matter which possesses this movement, and which is found everywhere, producing its effect whenever it finds a suitable disposition. Such also, as I conceive it, is the force of the <term>Spring</term> <note>Elasticity or "ressort"</note>, as much in steel and other solid bodies as in that of the air. To which one can add that of the muscles of animals, which is explained very well by a <term>fermentation</term> <note>In 17th-century physiology, "fermentation" referred to chemical-mechanical reactions in the body believed to produce heat and motion.</note> that the fluid of the nerves causes in the blood; but from where will the force of the fermentation come, if not from some movement from without? The powerful action of <term>Frost</term> <note>The expansion of freezing water</note> does not seem conceivable either, if one does not have recourse to a violent impulse of some matter, which causes either the ice to expand by introducing other particles into it, or the bubbles that form there by increasing the air they contain. This is done with such violence that I have seen musket barrels burst in which water had been enclosed.

But to return to <term>Gravity</term> <note>original: "Pesanteur"</note>; the extreme speed of the matter that causes it serves further to explain how heavy bodies, in falling, always accelerate their movement, even when they have already acquired it to a very great degree of speed. For that of the fluid matter, still greatly surpassing the celerity of a cannonball, for example, which falls back from the air after having been fired perpendicularly; this ball, until the end of its fall, feels very nearly the same pressure...

pressure of this matter, and therefore its speed is continually increased. Whereas, if the matter had only a moderate movement, the ball, after having acquired as much, would no longer accelerate its fall; because otherwise it would be obliged to push this same matter to follow into its place with more speed than the matter would have for that purpose by its own movement.

One can finally find here the reason for the Principle that Galileo <note>Galileo Galilei (1564–1642), whose laws of falling bodies are being explained here through mechanical causes.</note> adopted to demonstrate the proportion of the acceleration of falling bodies; which is that their speed increases equally in equal times. For as bodies are pushed successively by the parts of the matter which strives to rise into their place, and which, as we have just seen, act continually upon them with the same force—at least in the falls that come under our experience—it is a necessary consequence that the increase in speeds be proportional to that of the times.

Thus I have explained, by a Hypothesis <term>Hypothesis: a scientific supposition used as a starting point for reasoning without proof of its absolute truth</term> which contains nothing impossible, why terrestrial bodies tend toward the center; why the action of gravity cannot be hindered by the interposition of any body among those we know; why the internal parts of every body all contribute to its weight; and finally why bodies, in falling, continually increase their speed, and that in the ratio of the times. These are the properties of gravity that had been observed until now.

There remains one more, which until now was believed to be no less certain; which is that heavy bodies are just as heavy in one place on the Earth as in another. This having been found otherwise, by observations made recently, it is worth examining from where this may proceed, and what the consequences are.

It is asserted that it has been found in Cayenne <note>Cayenne, in French Guiana. This refers to Jean Richer’s 1672 expedition, where he discovered that a pendulum clocks beat slower near the equator, implying gravity is weaker there.</note>, which is a country

From this it follows that, if one takes pendulums of equal length, the one in Cayenne <note>original: "Caiene"; a reference to Jean Richer's 1672 expedition to French Guiana</note> makes its swings a little more slowly than the one in Paris. The truth of the fact being established, one cannot doubt that it is a sure sign that heavy bodies descend more slowly in that country than in France. And as this diversity cannot be attributed to the thinness of the air, which is greater in the Torrid Zone—because that ought to cause a completely opposite effect—I do not see that there can be any other reason, except that the same body weighs less under the Equator than under climates further away from it. I recognized, as soon as this new phenomenon was communicated to us, that the cause could be attributed to the daily motion of the Earth: which, being greater in each country according as it approaches the Equinoctial line <note>The Equator</note>, must produce an effort proportioned to throwing bodies away from the center; and thereby take away from them a certain part of their gravity. And it is easy, by the things explained above, to know what fractional part this must be in bodies located under the Equator. For having found, as has been seen, that if the Earth turned 17 times faster than it does, the centrifugal force under the Equator would be equal to the entire gravity of a body, it must be that the motion of the Earth, such as it is now, takes away a part of the gravity which is to the entire gravity as 1 is to the square of 17, that is to say 289; because the forces of bodies to move away from the center around which they turn are to each other as the squares of their speeds, according to my 3rd Theorem on <term>Centrifugal Force</term> <note>original: "Vi Centrifuga"; Latin for "centrifugal force." This refers to Huygens' own groundbreaking work on circular motion.</note>. We shall therefore also see what part of the absolute weight is the centrifugal effort <note>The outward force caused by rotation</note> at D O U H. That is to say, the ratio of D K to D H will be known, as being composed of that of 289 to 1, and of E C to D O. But the angle H D K is also known, being equal to that of the Latitude of Paris, namely 48 degrees 51 minutes. Therefore, one will know the ratio of D K to K H, which is that of the absolute gravity <term>absolute gravity: the weight a body would have if the Earth were not rotating</term> of bodies to that which they have in Paris; and this is also the ratio of the length of the pendulum on the stationary Earth to the length it must have under this Parallel, according to what has already been said. And since the length of the seconds pendulum <note>A pendulum that takes exactly one second for each swing</note> is given in Paris, we will also know what the length of the seconds pendulum would be on the stationary Earth, and what their difference is, and by how much this difference is less than that <unclear>3/16</unclear> <note>likely 1/289 or a specific fractional line measurement</unclear> that we had found under the Equator.

To perform this calculation with ease, and without the calculus of triangles, it must be known—and we shall prove it at this time—that as the square of the radius E C is to the square of D O, the sine of the complement of the Latitude of Paris, so is <unclear>3/16</unclear> <note>referring to the fractional shortening</note>, the difference or shortening of the pendulum under the Equator, to the difference or shortening in Paris. Which is found by this to be <unclear>3/16</unclear> of the length of the pendulum on the stationary Earth, or under the Pole. And since the seconds pendulum in Paris is 3 feet 8 <unclear>1/2</unclear> lines <note>A "line" is an old unit of measurement, 1/12 of an inch</note>; it follows that the length of the pendulum on the stationary Earth, or under the Pole, would be 3 feet 9 <unclear>1/2</unclear> lines. From which, subtracting <unclear>3/16</unclear>, which makes 1 <unclear>1/2</unclear> lines, one will have the length of the seconds pendulum under the Equator as 3 feet 7 <unclear>1/2</unclear> lines. So that this pendulum would be shorter than that of Paris by <unclear>1/2</unclear> of a line; which is a little less than what was found at Cayenne by Mr. Richer, namely one line and a quarter.

But one cannot rely entirely on these first observations, for which no circumstances are seen marked. And even less, I believe, on those which are said to have

# DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE

For a clock, for example, which was well regulated at Paris, being transported to some place <column-break/> would lose about one minute and 5 seconds in 24 hours, as it is easy to calculate <note>original: "supputer"</note> according to the preceding reasoning: and so in proportion for each different degree of latitude. Where one will find that these delays, among themselves, follow quite precisely the same proportion as the diminutions of the length of the pendulum: and that the greatest delay, such as that of a clock under the Equator, when it had been regulated under the Pole, would be very nearly 2½ minutes per day. Having therefore calculated tables, one could correct, by their means, the movement of the clocks, and use them with the same certainty as if this movement were everywhere equal.

# ON GRAVITY.

seeking the reduction of the <term>Pendulum</term><note>The length of a pendulum required to beat seconds changes based on the strength of gravity.</note> in Paris (and it is the same thing in any other place whatsoever) when one knows the amount of this reduction under the <term>Equator</term>: let there be taken, in the same figure, *KF* equal to *KH*, and let *HG* be parallel to the axis *PQ*. It has been shown that *HD* is to *DK* as the effort to move away from the center <note>original: "l’effort à s’éloigner du centre"; this refers to centrifugal force.</note> at *D* or *H* is to the absolute weight on the immobile Earth. But as *EC* or *CD* is to *DO*—that is to say, as *GD* is to *HD*—so is the centrifugal effort at *E*, under the Equator, to that at *D*. Therefore, as *GD* is to *DK*, so will be the centrifugal effort at *E* to the absolute weight on the immobile Earth. And the line *GD* will be the shortening of the pendulum which is required under the Equator, according to what was said previously. But *FD* is the shortening at Paris; and *GD* is to *DF* as the square of *GD* is to the square of *DH*; because the smallness of the angle *DKH* causes *HF* to be considered as perpendicular to *GD*. The shortening under the Equator, therefore, compared to that which suits Paris, is as the square of *GD* to the square of *DH*; that is to say, as the square of *CD*, or of *EC*, to the square of *DO*. Which was to be demonstrated.

It remains to consider the angle *HKD* in the same figure, which marks by how much the lead weight *KH*, being at rest, declines from the perpendicular *KD*. Wherein I find that, under the Parallel of Paris, this angle is 5 minutes 54 seconds; and that it must be even a little larger at the 45th degree of <term>Latitude</term>.

This <term>declination</term><note>The deviation of a plumb line from the true geometric center of the Earth.</note> is quite contrary to what has been supposed, for all time, as a very certain truth; namely, that the cord which holds a suspended lead weight tends directly to the center of the Earth. And this angle, of a tenth of a degree, is significant enough to make one believe that it should have been noticed, either in Astronomical observations or in those made with a <term>Level</term><note>An instrument used to determine a horizontal line, which relies on the verticality of a plumb line.</note>. For to speak only of these latter,

<summary>The author provides a geometric proof for calculating the shortening of a pendulum at different latitudes due to centrifugal force and notes that a plumb line does not point directly to the Earth's center.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's discourse on the physical causes of gravity and the shape of the Earth, specifically addressing the deviation of the plumb line mentioned in previous sections.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE</header>

would it not be necessary that, when looking toward the North, the line of the level would visibly drop below the <term>Horizon</term> <note>The author is arguing that if the Earth were a perfect sphere, the centrifugal force of its rotation would cause a visible discrepancy in leveling instruments</note>? This, however, has never been observed, nor does it certainly happen. And to give the reason for it, which is another paradox, it is because the <term>Earth</term> <note>Latin: Terra</note> is not entirely spherical, but of a spherical shape flattened toward the two <term>Poles</term>, much like an <term>Ellipse</term> <note>A flattened circle; here referring to an oblate spheroid</note> would be when rotating on its minor axis. This proceeds from the daily motion of the <term>Earth</term>, and it is a necessary consequence of the aforementioned declination of the <term>plumb</term> <note>The lead weight used to determine a vertical line</note>. Because the descent of heavy bodies is parallel to the line of this suspension, the surface of any liquid must dispose itself in such a way that this line is perpendicular to it; otherwise, it could descend further. Consequently, the surface of the <term>sea</term> is such that in every place the suspended thread is perpendicular to it. From which it follows that the line of the level—that is to say, the one that cuts the thread of the suspended <term>plumb</term> at right angles—must mark the <term>horizon</term>, just as it does: there being only the height of the place where the level is positioned that makes it aim slightly higher. Now, the coasts of the lands being generally elevated, and almost everywhere the same in regard to the <term>sea</term>, it follows that the whole composite of lands and seas is reduced to the same <term>spheroid</term> shape that the surface of the <term>sea</term> necessarily assumes. And it is to be believed that the <term>Earth</term> took this shape when it was assembled by the effect of gravity, its matter having possessed the circular motion of 24 hours since that time.

# DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE

Because in the experiment <note>original: "effay"</note> I have just mentioned, the experience agreed so well with what I had found through reasoning, I trust it enough to wish to continue this speculation <note>In the 17th century, "speculation" referred to deep theoretical study or contemplation rather than mere guessing.</note> by first seeking what the shape of the Earth is, since, as has been said, it is not spherical.

For this purpose, it is useful to consider the Earth as being entirely covered with water, or as if its entire mass were nothing else. And then it appears, by what has been explained above, that the surface must be such that, in whatever place it may be, the string supporting a lead weight <note><term>plumb line</term>: a cord with a weight attached, used to find a vertical reference line</note> meets the surface at right angles, taking into account both gravity <note>original: "pefanteur"</note> and the centrifugal force, which turns the string away from its direction toward the center. Because if the string did not meet the surface at right angles, the water could not remain in the position where it is.

Assuming then the same things as in the last figure of the preceding discourse, and also what was explained therein; but making the shape of the Earth slightly diminished and flattened toward the Poles, so that the axis PQ is shorter than the diameter EA; let BDSR be drawn parallel to KH, cutting EA and PQ at S and R. Since the string KH which supports the lead weight—or rather its parallel BD—must meet the surface of the sea at right angles; and since this string hangs in such a way that KD is to DH, or DC is to CS,

# ON GRAVITY.

C S, as the absolute gravity <note>The term "absolute gravity" refers to the force of gravity acting toward the center, independent of other forces.</note> is to the centrifugal force at D; which ratio is composed of that of the absolute gravity to the centrifugal force at E, which is as 289 to 1, and of that of this force to the centrifugal force at D, which is as E C to D O; it appears that the nature of the curved line B E D P is determined by the property of its perpendicular, such as D R; that is to say, that by always drawing such a perpendicular, the ratio of d c to c s must be composed of a given ratio, and of that of B E C to D O. Or else, as one can easily infer from it, that the ratio of d e to d c s, or of O R to R C must be composed of the said given ratio, and of that of B E C to C D.

Now it is difficult to find curved lines in this way by the given property of their perpendiculars, or, what is the same thing, by the property of their tangents. But there is a fairly easy means for this curve here, which is based on the equilibrium of certain canals, of which Mr. Newton <note>Sir Isaac Newton, whose *Principia* (1687) first proposed the Earth's shape as an oblate spheroid.</note> gave the first idea.

The canal that he supposes is represented in our figure by R C P, making a right angle at the center of the Earth. It must be conceived as having some small hollow, and filled with water. This being so, it is certain that the two legs, E C, C P, must hold themselves in equilibrium, if one supposes that the Earth, being entirely composed of water, takes a shape whose diameters are E A and P Q: because otherwise, this water of the canal would no longer remain in its position even when conceiving it without the canal, contrary to what is supposed. From which it is easy to find the ratio of E A to P Q. For by setting E C = α; C P = β, and representing the absolute gravity by a line p; and the centrifugal force at E by the line n; the weight of the canal p C is p b, namely that which is done by multiplying all the parts of this canal equally by the line p. # DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE
<note>The full title is "Discourse on the Cause of Gravity" (1690) by Christiaan Huygens.</note>

The weight, *pa*, is diminished by the centrifugal force <term>centrifugal force: the outward force experienced by a body moving in a circle</term> of all its parts, of which the highest, located at E, has the force *n*; and all the other parts have a force proportional to this one, according to their distances from the center D. This results in *na* for the total centrifugal force of the water in the canal E C, which, being subtracted from its weight *pa*, leaves *pa-na*, which must be equal to *pb*, the weight of the canal P C. From this, it appears that *a* is to *b* as *p* is to *p-n*. That is to say, the diameter E A of the Earth is to its axis P Q as 289 is to 288; or as 578 to 577; for the ratio of *p* to *n* was as 289 to 1.

I have assumed throughout this reasoning that gravity
is the same inside the Earth as at its surface, which seems
to me very likely, notwithstanding the reason one might have
to doubt it, of which I shall speak later. But even if it were
otherwise, that would change almost nothing in what has been found
regarding the figure of the Earth: except in cases where the centrifugal
force makes up a considerable part of gravity, or where it
is equal to it, as in the case of the Parabolic figure, which
would then become something entirely different. Furthermore, when the centrifugal force
at E is very small in proportion to gravity, as it is here
on Earth, the Hyperbola E T P, because of the great distance
of its center, closely approaches the Parabola, and consequently
E D P differs little from the Ellipse; nor much from the circle,
because E C then exceeds C P by only a very small amount; as it was
found shortly before, that this excess is only 1/300 of E C, the semi-diameter 
of the Earth.

Mr. Newton finds it to be 1/230 of E C, and thus that the figure
of the Earth differs much more from the spherical; employing in this
an entirely different calculation, which I shall not examine here,
because I also do not agree with a Principle that he
supposes in this calculation and elsewhere; which is, that all the small
parts, which one can imagine in two or several different
bodies, attract each other or tend to approach one another mutually.
This I could not admit, because I believe I see clearly
that the cause of such an attraction is not explainable
by any principle of Mechanics, nor by the rules of
motion. Just as I am not persuaded either of the necessity 
of the mutual attraction of whole bodies; having shown
that, even if there were no Earth, bodies would not
cease, by what we call their gravity, to tend
toward a center. <note>The author is likely Christiaan Huygens, who argued for a mechanical explanation of gravity (vortices of matter) rather than Newton's "action at a distance."</note>

<meta>The author continues his critique and synthesis of gravitational theories, specifically addressing the transition from Cartesian vortices to Newtonian mechanics.</meta>

# DISCOURSE ON THE CAUSE

I have nothing, therefore, against the <term>Centripetal Force</term> <note>original: "Vis Centripeta"</note>, as Mr. Newton calls it, by which he makes the Planets weigh toward the Sun, and the Moon toward the Earth; rather, I agree with it without difficulty. This is because we not only know by experience that there is such a manner of attraction or impulse in nature, but also because it is explained by the laws of motion, as was seen in what I wrote above concerning gravity. For nothing prevents the cause of this Centripetal Force toward the Sun from being similar to that which pushes bodies, which we call heavy, to descend toward the Earth. For a long time, I had imagined that the spherical figure of the Sun could be produced in the same way as that which, in my view, produces the sphericity of the Earth; but I had not extended the action of gravity to such great distances, such as from the Sun to the Planets, nor from the Earth to the Moon. This was because the <term>Vortices</term> <note>The "Tourbillons" of Descartes: a theory that space is filled with a fluid matter swirling like whirlpools, carrying the planets along.</note> of Mr. Descartes, which had formerly seemed very plausible to me and which I still had in mind, stood in the way. Nor had I thought of that regular diminution of gravity—namely, that it was in reciprocal proportion to the squares of the distances from the center—which is a new and very remarkable property of gravity, the reason for which is well worth seeking. But seeing now, by the demonstrations of Mr. Newton, that by supposing such a gravity toward the Sun, and which diminishes according to the said proportion, it counterbalances so well the centrifugal forces of the Planets and produces exactly the effect of the Elliptical motion which Kepler had guessed and verified by observations, I can hardly doubt that these Hypotheses concerning gravity are true, nor that Mr. Newton's System, insofar as it is founded thereupon, is likewise true. This must appear all the more probable as one finds therein the solution to several difficulties which caused trouble in the 
->Vortices<-

### ON GRAVITY.

<note>The author is comparing René Descartes' theory of "vortices"—fluid whirlpools of celestial matter—with Isaac Newton's theory of universal gravitation.</note> Descartes' supposed vortices. We now see how the eccentricities <note>The deviation of an orbit from a perfect circle.</note> of the Planets can remain constantly the same: why the planes of their Orbits do not merge, but keep their different inclinations with respect to the plane of the Ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky.</note>, and why the planes of all these Orbits necessarily pass through the Sun. How the movements of the Planets can accelerate and slow down by the degrees observed there; which could hardly be so if they swam in a Vortex around the Sun. Finally, one sees there how Comets can cross our System. For since it has been known that they often enter the region of the Planets, it was difficult to conceive how they could sometimes move in a direction contrary to that of the Vortex, which had enough force to carry away the Planets. But, by the doctrine of Mr. Newton, this doubt <note>original: "scrupule"</note> is further removed; since nothing prevents Comets from traveling in Elliptical paths around the Sun, just like the Planets; but in paths that are more extended, and of a shape more different from a circle; and thus these bodies have their periodic returns, as some ancient and modern Philosophers and Astronomers had imagined.

There is only this difficulty: that Mr. Newton, in rejecting the Vortices of Descartes, maintains that the celestial spaces contain only a very rare <note>Thin or low-density.</note> matter, so that the Planets and Comets encounter that much less obstacle in their course. This rarity being assumed, it does not seem possible to explain either the action of Gravity or that of Light, at least by the means I have used. To examine this point, then, I say that the ethereal matter can be considered rare in two ways: namely, either that its particles are distant from one another, with much vacuum between them; or that they touch, but that the fabric of each one is rare, and inter-

intermingled with many small empty spaces. As for the vacuum <note>original: "le vuide"</note>, I admit it without difficulty, and even believe it necessary for the movement of small corpuscles <term>corpuscles: tiny, indivisible particles of matter</term> among themselves. I do not share the opinion of Mr. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), who argued that a vacuum was impossible because space and matter were the same thing</note>, who maintains that extension alone constitutes the essence of body; rather, I add to it a perfect hardness, which renders it impenetrable and incapable of being broken or chipped. However, considering the rarity <note>density or thinness of matter</note> of the first manner, I do not see how one could then give an account of Gravity <note>original: "Peſanteur"</note>: and as for Light, it seems to me entirely impossible, with such vacuums, to explain its prodigious speed, which must be six hundred thousand times greater than that of Sound, according to the demonstration of Mr. Romer <note>Ole Rømer (1644–1710), the Danish astronomer who first measured the speed of light in 1676</note>, which I reported in the *Treatise on Light*. This is why I hold that such rarity cannot be suited to the celestial spaces.

There is more likelihood of conceiving it in the other way; because the particles can touch each other there, as I supposed in the said Treatise, and yet, because of the lightness of their texture, offer very little resistance to the movement of the Planets. For who knows how far nature can go in composing hard bodies with little matter; especially if very minute and delicate, or even hollow, particles can be infinitely strong. But I believe that, without considering rarity, the great agitation of the ethereal matter <term>ethereal matter: a subtle fluid then thought to fill the universe and transmit light and gravity</term> can contribute much to its penetrability. For if the slight movement of the particles of water makes it liquid, and of much less resistance regarding the bodies swimming within it than is sand or some very fine powder; must not a more subtle matter, and infinitely more agitated, also be that much easier to penetrate?

Be that as it may, we see that nature does not lack industry in creating spaces in which bodies move with very little resistance; for that ap-

As for the reasoning of Mr. <term>Newton</term> <note>Sir Isaac Newton (1643–1727), whose *Principia* challenged the mechanical explanations of gravity favored by the author.</note> in Proposition 6 of Book 3 to prove the extreme rarity of the <term>ether</term> <note>A hypothetical fluid once thought to fill all space and transmit light and gravity.</note>: namely, that the <term>weights</term> <note>original: "pesanteurs"</note> of bodies are proportional to the quantities of matter they contain; and that, this being the case, if the spaces of the air or the ether were as full of matter as gold and silver, these metals would not descend therein; because a solid body, not having a greater specific gravity than a fluid, would not be able to sink into it. I say that I agree that the weights of bodies follow the quantities of their matter; and I have even demonstrated this in the present Discourse. But I have also shown that for these bodies we call heavy, weight can indeed be impressed by the <term>centrifugal force</term> <note>The outward force felt by an object moving in a circle; the author argues gravity is caused by a swirling fluid pushing objects inward.</note> of a matter which does not itself weigh toward the center of the Earth because of its circular and very rapid movement, but which tends to move away. This matter, therefore, can very well fill all the space around the Earth that other corpuscles do not occupy, without this preventing the descent of the bodies we call heavy; being, on the contrary, the sole cause that compels them to it. It would be another matter if one supposed that gravity was an inherent quality of <term>corporeal matter</term>. But this is something I do not believe Mr. Newton would consent to, because such a hypothesis would lead us far away from <term>Mathematical or Mechanical principles</term>.

Because I do not deny that, when the Sun shines through a window, some motion spreads to the side of the illuminated space; but I say that these diverted waves are too weak to produce light.

And although he <note>Referring to Newton.</note> maintains that the emanation of Sound proves that these lateral spreadings are perceptible, I hold for certain that it proves the contrary instead. Because if Sound, having passed through an opening, spread to the side as Mr. Newton claims, it would not maintain so exactly, in an **Echo**, the equality of the angles of incidence and reflection; so that when one is placed in a spot from which no perpendicular line can fall upon the reflecting plane of a somewhat distant wall, one does not hear the Echo respond to the noise made in that place, as I have experimented very often. I also do not doubt that the experiment he brings forward regarding Sound—which one would hear despite an interposed house—would be found quite different, provided that this house were placed in the middle of some large body of water, or in such a way that there was nothing around it that could send back any particle of the Sound by reflection.

I first examined these movements by supposing that the forces of Resistance are proportional to the Velocities of the bodies, which at the time seemed very plausible to me. But having obtained what I was seeking, I learned almost at the same time, through the experiments we conducted in Paris at the Academy of Sciences, that the resistance of air and water was proportional to the squares of the velocities. And the reason is easy enough to conceive; because a body, moving for example with double velocity, is encountered by twice as many particles of air or water, and with double celerity. Thus I saw my new Theory overturned, or at least useless. After which I also wished to seek what happens when one supposes this true foundation of Resistances; where I saw that the matter was much more difficult, especially regarding the curved line traversed by bodies thrown obliquely <note>The author is referring to the trajectory of a projectile, which follows a parabola in a vacuum but a more complex curve in a resisting medium.</note>.

> namely, that a body, in falling through the air, continually increases its speed, but nevertheless in such a way that it can never exceed, nor even reach, a certain degree, which is the speed that the air would need to blow from bottom to top to hold the body suspended without being able to descend; for then, the force of the air against this body equals its weight. I call this speed, in each body, the <term>Terminal velocity</term> <note>The maximum constant speed an object reaches when the resistance of the medium prevents further acceleration.</note>.

vertical <note>original: "rité," likely "priorité" or "vélocité"</note> is the same. This is why each figure of a throw <note>term: jet (the path or trajectory of a thrown object)</note>, thus found, must be reduced to a proportional figure of equal height, if one wishes to know how the amplitudes and the heights of various throws relate to one another.

I add here further, that the Logarithmic line <note>term: Logarithmique (referring to a logarithmic curve)</note> does not only serve to find the curves of the throws, but that it is this curve itself in one case: namely, when one throws a body obliquely downward, in such a way that the perpendicular descent equals the Terminal velocity <note>term: viteffe Terminale (the constant maximum speed an object reaches when the resistance of the medium prevents further acceleration)</note>. For then this body will precisely follow the curvature of such a line, always approaching the asymptote without ever being able to reach it. And what determines the type of the line is that its *Subtangent* <note>term: Soutangente (the segment of the axis between the ordinate and the tangent line)</note> (I shall thus name the line FO, which is the same for all tangents) will be double the height to which the Terminal velocity can make the body rise, without resistance from the medium.

These are the things that I found by supposing the resistance to be as the velocity; but this entire Theory being, as I have said, founded on a principle that nature does not follow regarding the resistances of air and water, I neglected it entirely; and it is only on the occasion of Mr. Newton's Treatise <note>A reference to Isaac Newton's *Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica*, published in 1687.</note> that I took it up again, to see if what we had sought by very different ways agreed together as it should. Which is found to be so: for the construction for the line of the throw, which he gives in Proposition 4 of the 2nd Book, although entirely different from mine and more difficult, nevertheless produces the same curve, as can be proven by demonstration.

In examining what happens in the true hypothesis of Resistance, which is in duplicate ratio to the Velocity <note>note: This refers to the "square of the velocity," a more accurate model for fluid resistance at higher speeds.</note>, I had only determined this particular case: of a body thrown upward with its Terminal velocity, namely that the time of its entire elevation in the air is to the time it would employ to rise to where

<summary>The author discusses the logarithmic curve of a projectile's path under resistance and confirms that his mathematical models align with those published in Newton's Principia. He notes that while he initially modeled resistance as proportional to velocity, he is now shifting to the more accurate "square of the velocity" model.</summary>

it can without resistance, like the <term>Circle</term> <note>original: "Cercle"</note> to the <term>Square</term> <note>original: "Quarré"</note> which is circumscribed around it. And that the height of the first throw is to the height of the other, as the space between a <term>Hyperbola</term> <note>original: "Hyperbol"</note> and its <term>asymptote</term> <note>a line that a curve approaches but never meets</note>, terminated by two parallels to the other <term>asymptote</term> which are in a ratio of 2 to 1, is to the rectangle or parallelogram of the same <term>Hyperbola</term>. That is to say, as in the following figure, the space A M D K is to the <term>square</term> A C. I had not researched the other cases, which are included universally in Proposition 9 of the 2nd Book of Mr. <term>Newton</term>, which is very beautiful: and what prevented me from doing so was that I did not find, by the path I was following, the measurement of the descents of bodies, except by supposing the <term>quadrature</term> <note>the process of determining the area of a curved figure</note> of a certain <term>Curved line</term>, which I did not know depended on the <term>quadrature</term> of the <term>Hyperbola</term>. I reduced the dimension of the space of this curve to an <term>infinite Progression</term> <note>original: "Progreffion infinie"</note>, a + ½a³ + ¼a⁵ + ⅛a⁷ etc. Not knowing that the same <term>progression</term> also gave the measurement of the <term>Hyperbolic sector</term>: which I have seen since, by comparing the demonstration of Mr. Newton with what I had found.

But because this <term>Progression</term>, for the measurement of the Hyperbola, has not yet been remarked upon as far as I know, I wish to explain here how it serves that purpose. Let A B be a <term>Hyperbola</term>, of which the <term>asymptotes</term> D C, C E, make a right angle. Let the semi-axis be C A, perpendicular to D A E which touches the <term>Hyperbola</term>; and let A C B be a <term>Sector</term>, the line C B cutting A D at F. If one now takes A C or A D for the unit, and if A F is named *a*, which is a fraction less than unity, when A F, A D [are] not commensurable; I say that, as the sum of the <term>infinite Progression</term> a + ½a³ + ¼a⁵ + ⅛a⁷, etc. is to 1, so will the <term>Sector</term> A C B be to the <term>triangle</term> A C D. Or if one draws the perpendiculars A K, B L onto the <term>asymptote</term>, one can say the same thing of the space A B L K, which is equal to this <term>Sector</term>, as one sees easily by the equality of the <term>triangles</term> C A K, C B L. So that this Progression

# ON GRAVITY.

The progression for the Hyperbola <note>A type of smooth curve formed by the intersection of a double cone with a plane</note> corresponds to that given by Mr. Leibniz <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a prominent mathematician and philosopher who co-invented calculus</note> for the Circle. By this method, if the Sector of the Circle is ACG, having AC as its radius, and CG intersects AE at H; with AH being named *a*, and AE equal to *1*; the sum of the Progression *a* - ⅓*a*³ + ⅕*a*⁵ - ⅐*a*⁷ etc. <note>original: "a - ½a³ + ¼a⁵ - ⅛a⁷" - The text contains a slight printing error in the denominators of the Leibniz series for the arctangent, which should be odd numbers</note> is to *1*, as the Sector ACG is to the triangle ACE, or as the Arc AG is to the straight line AE.

Regarding the path of an oblique projectile <note>The trajectory of an object thrown at an angle</note>; if it were sufficient, in this type of resistance, to know the horizontal and vertical motion of a body in order to compose its oblique motion—just as in the first hypothesis—there would be a way to determine the points through which this line must pass. The same Logarithmic line would be useful there, being turned in such a way that its asymptote <note>A line that a curve approaches but never touches</note> was parallel to the horizon, and it would once again be the curve of the throw, in the case where I said it served previously. But this composition of motion does not take place here; because the decrease of retarded motion along the diagonal of a rectangle is not proportional to the decreases along the sides; it is extremely difficult, if not entirely impossible, to solve this Problem.

Horizontal motion, when considered on its own—such as a ball rolling on a smooth floor—has this remarkable quality here: that it must travel infinitely far, notwithstanding the resistance of the

of the medium; whereas, when the resistance is proportional to the velocity, it is limited and never reaches a certain limit. And this infinity is easily proven by Proposition 5 of the 2nd Book of Mr. Newton's Treatise <note>Isaac Newton's *Philosophiae Naturalis Principia Mathematica*, published in 1687</note>, because the space comprised between the Hyperbola and its asymptotes is of infinite magnitude.

The properties of the Logistic line <note>Now commonly known as the logarithmic curve</note>, which I promised to report, and some of which served to find what I have noted regarding motions through the air, are the following; besides the first, which I have already indicated, regarding the proportionality of the <term>ordinates</term> <note>The vertical coordinates or distances from a point on the curve to the horizontal axis</note> to the <term>asymptote</term> <note>A line that a curve approaches continually but never meets</note>, when they are equally distant, by which one finds points along this line.

1. That the spaces comprised between two ordinates to the asymptote are to each other as the differences of those ordinates.

2. That the same things being established, and AO being the <term>tangent</term> at point A, which cuts CE and QK at I and G; the spaces AVE and ADK are to each other as the straight lines VI and DG.

When I say that an infinite space has a certain ratio to a finite space, this means that it approaches so closely to the magnitude of a given space (which has that proportion to the finite space) that the difference can become less than any given space. In the preceding figure, the space A B Q D is to the infinite space—which extends from D Q between the curve and the <term>asymptote</term><note>A line that a curve approaches continually but never meets within a finite distance.</note>—as K D is to D Q.

7. That the infinite space between an ordinate <note>The vertical coordinate (y-axis) of a point on a curve</note>, the Logistic curve, and its asymptote <note>A line that a curve approaches continually but never meets</note>, on the side where these last two approach one another, is double the triangle formed by the ordinate, the tangent drawn from the same point as the ordinate, and the subtangent <note>The segment of the axis between the ordinate and the tangent</note>. Thus, in the same figure, the infinite space starting from the ordinate B F is double the triangle B F O.

8. That the space comprised between two ordinates is equal to the rectangle of the subtangent and the difference between those same ordinates. Thus, in the same figure, the space A D F B is equal to the rectangle of the subtangent F O and K A.

9. That the solid <note>A three-dimensional shape created by rotating a two-dimensional area</note> created by the infinite space starting from an ordinate, when rotating around the asymptote, is sesquialter <note>A ratio of 1.5 to 1, or one and a half times</note> to the Cone whose height is equal to the subtangent and the semi-diameter of the base is equal to the same ordinate. Thus, the solid created by the infinite space B F O C, rotating around F O, is sesquialter to the cone created by the triangle B F O rotating around the same F O.

10. That the solid produced by the same infinite space, when rotating around the ordinate B F from which it begins, is six times the cone created by the triangle B F O through its conversion <note>Rotation</note> upon B F. From which measurement of solids it follows:

11. That the center of gravity of the infinite space, starting from an ordinate, is distant from that ordinate by the length of the subtangent.

12. That this same center of gravity is distant from the asymptote by one-quarter of the ordinate.

13. I had also found that the center of gravity of the first of the said infinite solids is distant from its base by half of the subtangent.

14. And that the center of gravity of the other solid is distant from its infinite base by one-eighth of its axis.

15. It is well enough known that this Logistic line serves for Quadrature <note>The process of determining the area of a surface or finding a square with the same area as a curvilinear figure</note>.

the quadrature <note>The process of determining the area of a geometric figure</note> of the Hyperbola, following the demonstrations of Father Gregory of St. Vincent <note>Grégoire de Saint-Vincent (1584–1667), a Jesuit mathematician known for his work on conic sections</note> regarding the hyperbolic spaces contained between two ordinates <note>The vertical coordinate or distance from an axis</note> on one of the asymptotes <note>A line that a curve approaches but never touches</note>. <image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating a hyperbola. The diagram shows two intersecting lines, labeled as asymptotes, with a curve representing the hyperbola. But it has not been noted, as far as I know, that these same hyperbolic spaces are to the Parallelogram of the Hyperbola (I call the parallelogram whose sides are the two ordinates on the asymptotes, drawn from the same point of the Section, by this name) as each of the lines DG, FE, is to the subtangent <term>subtangent: a segment of the axis of a curve that helps determine the slope or tangent at a specific point</term> FO. So that, if the Parallelogram of the Hyperbola is assumed to be 0.4342944819 parts, each hyperbolic space, contained between two ordinates to one of the asymptotes, will be to this parallelogram as the Logarithm of the proportion of the same ordinates; that is to say, as the difference of the Logarithms of the numbers which express the proportion of the ordinates to the number 0.4342944819; taking Logarithms of 10 characters besides the characteristic <note>The integer part of a logarithm</note>.
